violation rate, motivating type-level analysis as a diagnostic rather than predictive tool.\"\n\n这样把 variance decomposition 和 NMI 合并为 contribution (2) 的核心，taxonomy repositioning 变为推导结论而非独立贡献。\n\n### N3: Taxonomy 降格 — 释放 ~2 行\n\n**§1 contributions**：当前 contribution (3) 描述 error analysis (~65 words)。压缩——systematic nocuity 和 disambiguation neglect 不再列为独立 contribution，改为 \"(3) an error analysis revealing type-specific failure mechanisms and convergent violation patterns across models.\"\n\n**§3 (taxonomy.tex) 不动**——已是方法论写法。\n\n**§2 (related_work.tex) 末段**：加 1 句强调 taxonomy 是 experimental instrument：\"Our taxonomy serves as an experimental instrument for controlled causal analysis rather than a proposed classification standard.\"\n\n### W7: Abstract 数字精简 — 释放 ~2-3 行\n\n**保留**（7 个核心数字）：300 pairs, five models, 34.0pp, OR=15.1, 22.4% vs 0.4%, 17×\n**删除**：2997 episodes, 1.0% model variance, +49.2/+18.7/+30.8-36.0pp (C2 demoted), 614 traces, 4.6%\n**保留 ≤11%**（\"even the best 行。\n\n---\n\n## 二、Framing 调整（不影响空间或已在上面处理）\n\n### N2: Judge-model confound C4\n\n§5.4 (experiments.tex ~line 66)：\n当前 \"we characterize this as suggestive (§Limitations)\"\n→ 改为 \"we characterize this ```text
1	\section{Error Analysis \emph{[Exploratory]}}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	The following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy}).
5	
6	We analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes (Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$, $\kappa = 0.52$ \citep{gwet_2014,cohen_1960}). Using LLMs to code LLM failures introduces circularity risk; however, mutual information between type (exogenous) and mechanism labels ($I(\text{type}; \text{mechanism}) = 0.41$ bits, $V = 0.45$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}) provides structural evidence against this.
7	
8	\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}
9	
10	The five-model data reveal three mechanistically distinct failure patterns that crosscut the specification/linguistic layer boundary.
11	
12	\emph{Structural misparse}: the agent constructs an incorrect parse and follows it consistently, paralleling garden-path processing \citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002} at a behavioral level. Dominates scopal (5/5 models), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5).
13	
14	\emph{Information gap-filling}: the agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults. Dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5).
15	
16	\emph{Boundary violation}: the agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified; 69.2\% involve unauthorized escalation with zero conservative refusals ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial). Dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).
17	
18	Each type's dominant failure mode is majority-consistent ($\geq$3/5 models), with two achieving unanimity (scopal and authorization scope).
19	
20	\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}
21	
22	Agents rarely recognize ambiguity. We code \emph{disambiguation awareness} versus \emph{surface adoption} (proceeding with a single interpretation). Overall awareness is 4.6\% ($\leq$11\% per model); Qwen3 and DeepSeek show zero across all 278 combined traces.
23	
24	We term this \emph{Disambiguation Neglect}---the absence of disambiguation in default settings, not inability. Prior work shows LLMs collapse ambiguity into single interpretations \citep{liu_ambiguity_2023,hu_ambiguity_2025}; we extend this to agentic contexts where the result is irreversible policy violation. Our mitigation experiment ($-$8.5\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms disambiguation can be elicited.
25	
26	\subsection{Systematic Nocuity}
27	
28	Among ambiguous clauses, 41.3\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\geq$3/5 models), degrading monotonically across thresholds ($\geq$2/5: 54.7\%, $\geq$4/5: 23.7\%, 5/5: 13.0\%)---a phenomenon we term \emph{systematic nocuity} \citep{berry_kamsties_2004}: models converge on the same incorrect interpretation, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. Of these, 83.9\% share the same dominant failure mode, confirming convergent---not coincident---violations. Incompleteness has the highest universal rate (24\%) and is disproportionately represented (OR $= 2.61$, $p = 0.019$).
29	
```

Our mitigation experiment ($-$8.5\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms disambiguation can be elicited.\n\n\\subsection{Systematic Nocuity}\n\nAmong ambiguous clauses, 41.3\\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\\geq$3/5 models), degrading monotonically across thresholds ($\\geq$2/5: 54.7\\%, $\\geq$4/5: 23.7\\%, 5/5: 13.0\\%)---a phenomenon we term \\emph{systematic nocuity} \\citep{berry_kamsties_2004}: models converge on the same incorrect interpretation, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. Of these, 83.9\\% share the same dominant failure mode, confirming convergent---not coincident---violations. Incompleteness has the highest universal rate (24\\%) and is disproportionately represented (OR $= 2.61$, $p = 0.019$).\n",

      "totalLines": 29
    }

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. We present a matched-pair causal study on $\tau^2$-bench with 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy, evaluated across five models (2,997 episodes). Ambiguous clauses are consistently nocuous (i.e., harmful), increasing violation rates by 34.0 percentage points (conditional OR $= 15.1$, matched-pair), with perturbation controls confirming specificity to semantic ambiguity. A variance decomposition reveals that individual clause wording explains 22.4\% of violation variance versus 0.4\% for ambiguity type and 1.0\% for model identity, establishing clause-level wording as the dominant risk factor. However, a normalized mutual information analysis shows that type membership predicts failure \emph{mechanism} 17$\times$ more strongly than it predicts violation rate, establishing the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool for failure-mechanism prediction rather than risk prediction. Per-type effects form a partial ordering with robust extremes: incompleteness as the consistent upper bound ($+$49.2\,pp, conditional on an information-addition confound) and coreferential as the lower bound ($+$18.7\,pp), and a margin-dependent mid-tier structure spanning $+$30.8 to $+$36.0\,pp. Error analysis of 614 violation traces reveals \emph{Disambiguation Neglect} ($\leq$11\% per-model awareness) and \emph{systematic nocuity}: 41.3\% of clauses triggering model-convergent violations. These results, observed in customer-service policy domains, motivate clause-level policy auditing and type-targeted diagnosis for policy-governed deployments.
3	\end{abstract}
4

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	A customer asks to return a customized item within the 30-day window. The agent consults the store policy, which states that ``items may be returned within 30 days.'' The word ``items'' scopes over all product categories, including custom orders that are normally final sale. The agent grants a full refund and arranges free return shipping. One word of scopal ambiguity costs the retailer \$200.
5	
6	LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that govern their permitted actions. These specifications inevitably contain ambiguity: a property that has been studied for decades in requirements engineering (RE) and computational linguistics. \citet{berry_kamsties_2004} distinguish between \emph{nocuous} (i.e., harmful) ambiguity, where different stakeholders arrive at different interpretations, and \emph{innocuous} ambiguity, where shared organizational context ensures convergent interpretation. In human RE workflows, most ambiguity is innocuous because developers share domain knowledge, can consult colleagues, and iteratively clarify requirements.
7	
8	LLM agents lack these disambiguation channels. They cannot consult colleagues, rarely escalate to users, and must resolve ambiguity in real time from the text alone. This raises a question that has not been empirically addressed: does policy ambiguity cause safety violations in LLM agents, and if so, does the \emph{type} of ambiguity predict the severity of the violation? Prior work classifies ambiguity types in policies \citep{drip_r_2026,li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} or measures policy understanding quality \citep{policybank_2026}, but no existing study provides a causal, type-granularity mapping from ambiguity to safety violations.
9	
10	\begin{figure*}[t]
11	\centering
12	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig1_overview.pdf}
13	\caption{Experimental overview. We construct 300 matched clause pairs across a six-type, two-layer ambiguity taxonomy and inject them into $\tau^2$-bench policy documents. Five models interact with the modified policies across 2,997 episodes. A cross-judge evaluation pipeline classifies violation severity on a four-tier scale.}
14	\label{fig:overview}
15	\end{figure*}
16	
17	We address this gap with a matched-pair causal study on $\tau^2$-bench \citep{tau2_bench_2025}, a dual-control conversational agent benchmark. Using a six-type taxonomy organized into two layers---linguistic and specification, including specification-level gaps that produce equivalent behavioral effects in LLM agents (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy}), we construct 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs and evaluate five architecturally diverse models across 2,997 episodes.
18	
19	Our results show that policy ambiguity is consistently nocuous for LLM agents ($+34.0$ pp violation rate increase, conditional OR $= 15.1$), but risk is driven by individual clause wording (22.4\% of variance) rather than type membership (0.4\%). The taxonomy's value lies in predicting failure mechanisms: type membership predicts the dominant mechanism 17$\times$ better than it predicts violation rate ($\text{NMI} = 0.23$ vs.\ $0.01$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}), enabling targeted diagnosis (\S\ref{sec:discussion}). Incompleteness is consistently the highest-risk type (46--62\% across all models); the remaining types form a partial ordering with robust extremes but margin-dependent mid-tier structure.
20	
21	Our contributions are: (1)~the first matched-pair causal study of policy ambiguity's safety impact with perturbation control (\S\ref{sec:method}), observed across five primary models and confirmed in two additional replication models; (2)~a variance decomposition showing that individual clause wording (22.4\%) dominates type membership (0.4\%) and model identity (1.0\%), combined with a mutual information analysis demonstrating that type predicts failure mechanism 17$\times$ better than violation rate (\S\ref{sec:analysis}, \S\ref{sec:discussion}), repositioning the taxonomy from a risk-prediction tool to a diagnostic taxonomy for failure-mechanism prediction; and (3)~an error analysis quantifying \emph{Disambiguation Neglect} in consequential agentic settings---extending LLM ambiguity-collapse findings \citep{liu_ambiguity_2023,hu_ambiguity_2025} to agentic settings---with $\leq$11\% per-model awareness (4.6\% overall), and \emph{systematic nocuity}: 41.3\% of ambiguous clauses trigger majority-convergent violations, indicating clause-level risk factors (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
22	
```

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We organize results around four analyses of increasing specificity: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2), the aggregate layer effect (C3), and the model$\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 constitute our primary claims; C3 is inconclusive; C4 is exploratory but provides the model$\times$type interaction analysis.
5	
6	\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}
7	
8	Across all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\% vs.\ 6.9\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity ($n = 300$, $\Delta_{\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\,pp, CI containing zero). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\footnote{Type III SS on binary outcomes approximates a linear probability model; logistic regression (Appendix~\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant factor ordering (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \citealp{tjur_2009}).} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\% (0.4\% between types, 22.0\% within), model 1.0\%, condition 15.8\%, residual 60.8\% (conversation trajectory, user-simulator stochasticity). A permutation test ($n = 1{,}000$, shuffling within type; Appendix~\ref{app:clause_variance}) confirms the clause effect exceeds chance (null mean $= 8.3\%$, $p < 0.001$); clause-level ICC $= 0.386$ (mixed-model $= 0.189$). Individual clause wording---not type membership---drives clause-level variance; the omnibus test on ambiguous episodes nonetheless confirms significant type heterogeneity ($\chi^2 = 28.02$, $V = 0.137$; Table~\ref{tab:main_results}). Among confound-controlled types, per-type effects range from $+$18.7\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\,pp (authorization scope); incompleteness reaches $+$49.2\,pp but is conditional on an information-addition confound (Appendix~\ref{app:confound}). A replication on two additional model families (Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}) confirms the effect across seven models ($\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\,pp, both $p < 10^{-6}$); excluding incompleteness, it remains large ($\Delta = 31.0$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:sans_incompleteness}). A cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency across all five models ($\Delta = +13.8$\,pp pooled; Appendix~\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy's primary value lies not in predicting clause risk but in predicting failure mechanisms: type predicts mechanism 17$\times$ more strongly than violation rate (\S\ref{sec:discussion}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.
9	
10	\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}
11	
12	\begin{table*}[t]
13	\centering
14	\caption{Violation rates (\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\% CIs. Tier: TOST equivalence cluster at $\pm 15$\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\pm 12$\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}
15	\label{tab:main_results}
16	\footnotesize
17	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}
18	\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\hskip 6pt}cr}
19	\toprule
20	Layer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\Delta$pp\,[95\% CI] & Tier & $p$ \\
21	\midrule
22	\multirow{3}{*}{\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\!10^{-37}$ \\
23	 & Auth.\ Scope & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\!10^{-22}$ \\
24	 & Cond.\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\!10^{-17}$ \\
25	\midrule
26	\multirow{3}{*}{\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\!10^{-20}$ \\
27	 & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\!10^{-21}$ \\
28	 & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\!10^{-6}$ \\
29	\midrule
30	\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{Overall}} & \textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \textbf{+34.0}\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\!10^{-105}$ \\
31	\bottomrule
32	\end{tabular}
33	\end{table*}
34	
35	The six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \times 10^{-5}$, Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). TOST equivalence testing at a $\pm 15$ pp margin reveals a graded equivalence structure among five types---authorization scope (41.6\%), lexical (42.8\%), scopal (39.4\%), and conditional precedence (36.8\%) form a mid-tier cluster, with coreferential (31.6\%) below---while incompleteness (53.6\%) stands apart as an upper bound---conditional on the confound controls in Appendix~\ref{app:confound}---whose ranking partly reflects an information-addition confound. Table~\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.
36	
37	Sensitivity analysis across seven margins ($\pm 5$--$20$ pp; Appendix~\ref{app:tost}) confirms this structure is margin-dependent: mid-tier cohesion emerges at $\pm 12$ pp and stabilizes at $\pm 15$ pp, while the incompleteness upper-bound is robust across all margins. A bootstrap cluster analysis ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\ref{app:cluster}) corroborates: incompleteness forms a singleton at $k = 2$, coreferential additionally separates at $k = 3$. Cross-model type ranking concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\ref{app:kendall_w}), driven by incompleteness ranking highest in four of five models.
38	
39	An important qualification: cross-judge-group type rankings are discordant ($\rho = 0.09$, $p = 0.87$; Appendix~\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), driven by authorization scope ranking first in one judge group but last in the other. Excluding it, the remaining five types show strong concordance ($\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$). C2 should be interpreted as establishing robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound---rather than a complete linear ordering.
40	
41	Incompleteness is highest in four of five models (rates $\geq$46\% in all seven; Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}). Its unambiguous variant adds policy content, so the 49.2\,pp effect is an upper bound; a confound analysis confirms significance after controlling for information delta (Appendix~\ref{app:confound}). Coreferential, whose variants are lexically identical, provides a confound-free lower bound at 31.6\%.
42	
43	\begin{figure}[t]
44	\centering
45	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}
46	\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\% CIs. Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\S\ref{sec:results}).}
47	\label{fig:forest_plot}
48	\end{figure}
49	
50	\subsection{Layer Effect (C3)}
51	
52	Specification-layer types trend higher than linguistic-layer types (44.0\% vs.\ 37.9\%, $p = 0.017$), but C3 is inconclusive: the effect does not survive Holm--Bonferroni correction, controlling for token delta reverses the direction, and cross-layer type overlap (conditional precedence 36.8\% vs.\ scopal 39.4\%) further undermines the distinction. Type-level distinctions (C2) hold independently of layer grouping. We accordingly treat the two-layer structure as organizational convenience rather than a theoretical claim.
53	
54	\subsection{Model $\times$ Type Interaction (C4) \emph{[Exploratory]}}
55	\label{sec:c4}
56	
57	\begin{figure}[t]
58	\centering
59	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_heatmap.pdf}
60	\caption{Violation rates (\%) by model and type under ambiguous conditions ($n = 2{,}997$). Authorization scope varies 52\,pp across models ($\kappa = 0.385$); incompleteness remains uniformly high (46--62\%). C4 interaction is authorization-scope-specific (excluding it: $p = 0.97$).}
61	\label{fig:heatmap}
62	\end{figure}
63	
64	A model main effect is significant ($\chi^2 = 31.02$, $p = 3.0 \times 10^{-6}$, $V = 0.102$), with Claude (30.3\%) substantially less vulnerable than DeepSeek (49.7\%). Model identity explains only 1.0\% of total violation variance; the significant interaction ($F(20, 2976) = 1.97$, $p = 0.006$; $\mathrm{df} = (5{-}1) \times (6{-}1) = 20$; 2.9\% of variance; confirmed by logistic regression likelihood ratio test: $\chi^2(20) = 47.20$, $p = 5.5 \times 10^{-4}$) concentrates in specific type--model combinations.
65	
66	Figure~\ref{fig:heatmap} highlights three patterns: (1) authorization scope is the \emph{most model-differentiating type} (52\,pp range; Claude 12\% to GPT-5.4 64\%), though its low judge agreement ($\kappa = 0.385$) adds uncertainty; (2) incompleteness is a \emph{consistent vulnerability} (46--62\%); (3) conditional precedence is the most \emph{model-invariant} (34--40\%). The authorization scope ranking is stable within the GPT-5.4 judge subset (bootstrap preservation: 99.5\%), and the 34\,pp within-judge range far exceeds measurement noise; given the low cross-judge $\kappa$, we characterize this as suggestive (\S\ref{sec:limitations}). Excluding authorization scope, the residual interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}).
67	
68

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Differential nocuity.}
5	Building on \citet{berry_kamsties_2004}'s nocuous/innocuous distinction, our results support a \emph{differential nocuity} hypothesis \citep{willis_nocuous_2008}: risk depends on type, safety training, and disambiguation channel availability. The binary effect is domain-robust (interaction $p = 0.187$) but type rankings are not ($\rho = 0.26$), suggesting nocuity is universal while its type-specific profile requires per-domain calibration.
6	
7	\paragraph{Taxonomy as diagnostic tool.}
8	Type membership explains only 0.4\% of violation variance versus 22.4\% for clause wording; however, it predicts failure \emph{mechanism} 17$\times$ more strongly than violation rate ($\text{NMI}$ ratio $= 16.8$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}), validating the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
9	
10	\paragraph{Prompt-based mitigation.}
11	A type-specific mitigation study (Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}) finds prompting effective for authorization scope ($-18.0$\,pp) and lexical ($-8.9$\,pp) but not incompleteness ($-3.3$\,pp, reversing across formulations), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes \citep{chantree_2006,ferrari_ambiguity_2019}.
12	
```

      "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Differential nocuity.}\nBuilding on \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}'s nocuous/innocuous distinction, our results support a \\emph{differential nocuity} hypothesis \\citep{willis_nocuous_2008}: risk depends on type, safety training, and disambiguation channel availability. The binary effect is domain-robust (interaction $p = 0.187$) but type rankings are not ($\\rho = 0.26$), suggesting nocuity is universal while its type-specific profile requires per-domain calibration.\n\n\\paragraph{Taxonomy as diagnostic tool.}\nType membership explains only 0.4\\% of violation variance versus 22.4\\% for clause wording; however, it predicts failure \\emph{mechanism} 17$\\times$ more strongly than violation rate ($\\text{NMI}$ ratio $= 16.8$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), validating the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-based mitigation.}\nA type-specific mitigation study (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) finds prompting effective for authorization scope ($-18.0$\\,pp) and lexical ($-8.9$\\,pp) but not incompleteness ($-3.3$\\,pp, reversing across formulations), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes \\citep{chantree_2006,ferrari_ambiguity_2019}.\n",

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	\paragraph{Scope.} We evaluate seven API-accessible models from six providers (Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}); smaller open-source models (7B--70B) may exhibit different vulnerability patterns. $\tau^2$-bench covers retail and airline customer service only---effects in other domains remain untested. Cross-domain consistency holds for the overall effect (C1, interaction $p = 0.19$) and model ranking ($\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$), but type rankings vary across domains ($\rho = 0.26$, $p = 0.62$); conditional precedence in particular differs by 22\,pp between retail and airline, further motivating the partial-ordering framing. Our matched-pair design requires synthetic ambiguity construction, which may not capture the full distribution of naturally occurring ambiguities; realism validation (\S\ref{sec:method}) confirms lexical comparability but cannot rule out distributional gaps. Coreferential ambiguity was excluded from mitigation and replication experiments due to benchmark-specific structural constraints (Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}); consequently, the confound-free lower bound ($+$18.7\,pp) rests on the primary five-model study alone.
5	
6	\paragraph{Measurement.} Each model is evaluated by a single cross-judge, confounding judge identity with model identity; within-judge-group analyses confirm C1 individually (both $p < 0.001$) but not C3 (Appendix~\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), and cross-model type ranking concordance is only moderate (Appendix~\ref{app:kendall_w}). Most critically, cross-judge-group type rankings show near-zero correlation ($\rho = 0.09$, $p = 0.87$; Appendix~\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), meaning that the C2 gradient's mid-tier ordering does not generalize across judge groups---though excluding authorization scope yields strong concordance ($\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$), and incompleteness as the highest-risk type is robust to this variation. Per-type reliability varies---authorization scope has the lowest agreement ($\kappa = 0.385$) and per-type human--LLM estimates rest on small cell sizes with wide confidence intervals (Appendix~\ref{app:per_type_agreement})---while failure mode coding achieves moderate agreement ($\kappa = 0.52$), with minority categories less reliable. Both judges belong to the OpenAI model family, so systematic leniency toward same-family outputs or severity toward competitors cannot be ruled out; cross-family judge evaluation (e.g., Claude judging GPT traces) would strengthen C4 conclusions. ICC is computed within the OpenAI model family (GPT-5.4 across runs); run-to-run variance for non-OpenAI models remains unmeasured. Failure mode coding relies on LLM annotators; although mutual information between exogenous type labels and LLM-coded mechanism labels ($I = 0.41$ bits) provides structural anti-circularity evidence, human validation of a failure-mode subset would further strengthen these findings.
7	
8	\paragraph{Confounds.} Incompleteness resolution necessarily adds policy content, creating an information asymmetry; our logistic regression controls and coreferential lower-bound analysis mitigate but do not eliminate this confound (\S\ref{sec:results}). Token-count difference between ambiguous and unambiguous variants is an imperfect proxy for informational asymmetry; future work could employ semantic complexity metrics. Our primary analysis uses a moderate-or-above severity threshold; the severity threshold was not pre-registered, but robustness to alternative thresholds is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:severity}. Switching to any-violation yields consistent results, with all four claims remaining significant and concordant type rankings.
9	
10	\paragraph{Disambiguation channel.} $\tau^2$-bench system prompts instruct agents toward helpfulness, potentially suppressing disambiguation behavior; the 4.6\% awareness rate may underestimate capacity in settings where clarification is explicitly encouraged.
11	
12	\section*{Data and Code Availability}
13	The 300 clause pairs, evaluation pipeline, analysis scripts, and raw episode traces will be released upon publication at \url{https://github.com/anonymous/policy-ambiguity-safety}.
14	
```

1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	Policy ambiguity increases agent violations by 34.0\,pp (OR\,$=$\,15.1); clause wording dominates variance (22.4\%) over type (0.4\%) and model (1.0\%), while the taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms 17$\times$ more strongly than violation rate. With $>$89\% disambiguation neglect and 41.3\% convergent violations, clause-level auditing is critical for policy-governed deployments.
4

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Ambiguity in NLP and requirements engineering.}
5	Ambiguity has been studied extensively in both computational linguistics and software engineering. Word sense disambiguation \citep{navigli_wsd_2009} addresses lexical ambiguity at the token level; our lexical type extends this to policy-specific polysemy. \citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} propose an 11-type NLP taxonomy covering syntactic, semantic, and pragmatic ambiguity, from which we draw our linguistic-layer types. \citet{kamath_scope_2024} investigate scopal ambiguity in LLMs, finding systematic preferences for surface scope. In requirements engineering (RE), \citet{massey_ambiguity_re_2014} classify six ambiguity types in regulatory texts, and \citet{berry_kamsties_2004} introduce the distinction between nocuous ambiguity (where stakeholders diverge on interpretation) and innocuous ambiguity (where they converge). Computational detection of nocuous ambiguity has progressed from heuristic methods \citep{chantree_2006,willis_nocuous_2008} through NLP-based cross-domain detectors \citep{ferrari_ambiguity_2019,ferrari_defects_2018} to transformer-based approaches for anaphoric ambiguity \citep{ezzini_taphsir_2022,ezzini_anaphoric_2022}. \citet{zhao_nlp4re_2021} survey the broader NLP4RE landscape. Our work extends Berry and Kamsties' framework to LLM agents, where the absence of shared organizational context makes previously innocuous ambiguity nocuous.
6	
7	\paragraph{Psycholinguistics of ambiguity processing.}
8	\citet{ferreira_good_enough_2002} show that human comprehenders build shallow, ``good-enough'' representations rather than full parses, paralleling our observation that LLM agents adopt surface interpretations. \citet{frazier_rayner_1982} demonstrate early commitment to a single parse; \citet{li_garden_path_llm_2024} provide direct evidence that LLMs exhibit analogous garden-path effects. At the pragmatic level, \citet{hu_pragmatic_2023} and \citet{ruis_goldilocks_2023} show that LLMs struggle with implicature resolution, defaulting to literal interpretations even when pragmatic inference is required. \citet{liu_ambiguity_2023} demonstrate that language models fail to represent ambiguity, collapsing multiple interpretations into a single output; \citet{gur_arieh_ambiguity_collapse_2026} taxonomize the epistemic risks of this ``ambiguity collapse''; \citet{sedova_to_know_2024} show analogous self-inconsistencies under entity-type ambiguity. These findings ground our \emph{Disambiguation Neglect} result: agents process ambiguous input without meta-cognitive awareness but, unlike humans, lack social channels to recover. \citet{disambiguation_cqa_survey_2025} survey disambiguation strategies in conversational QA; \citet{suri_structured_2025} show that structured uncertainty estimation improves coverage by 7--39\%, while \citet{sehwag_aspi_2026} demonstrate that clarification-seeking introduces prompt injection vulnerabilities---a tension for disambiguation channel design.
9	
10	\paragraph{Agent safety evaluation.}
11	The agent safety evaluation landscape includes benchmarks for risk awareness \citep{rjudge_2024}, tool-use safety \citep{toolemu_2024}, indirect prompt injection \citep{injecagent_2024}, safe task planning \citep{yin_safeagentbench_2025}, policy-invisible violations \citep{phantompolicy_2026}, comprehensive real-world agent safety \citep{vijayvargiya_openagentsafety_2026}, trajectory-level diagnosis \citep{atbench_2026}, and deployment-condition probing \citep{riskybench_2026}. \citet{tamkin_evaluating_2023} evaluate how language models navigate policy-governed decisions, \citet{shieldagent_2025} extract formal safety rules from policy documents, and AgentAuditor \citep{agentauditor_2025} achieves human-level safety evaluation. Our work differs by focusing on the \emph{source specification} rather than the agent's execution: we measure how ambiguity in the policy itself causes safety violations, complementing work that assumes clean policies.
12	
13	\paragraph{Policy specification for agents.}
14	DRIP-R \citep{drip_r_2026} is the closest concurrent work: it benchmarks 400 policy-ambiguous retail scenarios across five RE ambiguity types, measuring decision quality rather than safety violations. Our work differs in three respects: (1) our matched-pair design establishes causality rather than correlating ambiguity presence with outcomes; (2) we measure safety violations rather than decision quality, enabling failure mechanism analysis; (3) our two-layer taxonomy adds linguistic-layer types, and we quantify the model$\times$type interaction. We map DRIP-R's five RE ambiguity categories to our six-type taxonomy in Appendix~\ref{app:drip_mapping}. PolicyBank \citep{policybank_2026} studies iterative policy refinement, closing 82\% of the gap to a human oracle. AgentPex \citep{agentpex_2026} detects procedural violations in agentic traces, finding that 83\% of perfect-outcome traces contain violations. MANTRA \citep{mantra_2026} synthesizes SMT-validated compliance benchmarks from procedural manuals. $\tau$-bench \citep{tau_bench_2024} and its successor $\tau^2$-bench \citep{tau2_bench_2025} provide the conversational agent evaluation platforms. 
```

Our work extends Berry and Kamsties' framework to LLM agents, where the absence of shared organizational context makes previously innocuous ambiguity nocuous.\n\n\\paragraph{Psycholinguistics of ambiguity processing.}\n\\citet{ferreira_good_enough_2002} show that human comprehenders build shallow, ``good-enough'' representations rather than full parses, paralleling our observation that LLM agents adopt surface interpretations. \\citet{frazier_rayner_1982} demonstrate early commitment to a single parse; \\citet{li_garden_path_llm_2024} provide direct evidence that LLMs exhibit analogous garden-path effects. At the pragmatic level, \\citet{hu_pragmatic_2023} and \\citet{ruis_goldilocks_2023} show that LLMs struggle with implicature resolution, defaulting to literal interpretations even when pragmatic inference is required. \\citet{liu_ambiguity_2023} demonstrate that language models fail to represent ambiguity, collapsing multiple interpretations into a single output; \\citet{gur_arieh_ambiguity_collapse_2026} taxonomize the epistemic risks of this ``ambiguity collapse''; \\citet{sedova_to_know_2024} show analogous self-inconsistencies under entity-type ambiguity. These findings ground our \\emph{Disambiguation Neglect} result: agents process ambiguous input without meta-cognitive awareness but, unlike humans, lack social channels to recover. \\citet{disambiguation_cqa_survey_2025} survey disambiguation strategies in conversational QA; \\citet{suri_structured_2025} show that structured uncertainty estimation improves coverage by 7--39\\%, while \\citet{sehwag_aspi_2026} demonstrate that clarification-seeking introduces prompt injection vulnerabilities---a tension for disambiguation channel design.\n\n\\paragraph{Agent safety evaluation.}\nThe agent safety evaluation landscape includes benchmarks for risk awareness \\citep{rjudge_2024}, tool-use safety \\citep{toolemu_2024}, indirect prompt injection \\citep{injecagent_2024}, safe task planning \\citep{yin_safeagentbench_2025}, policy-invisible violations \\citep{phantompolicy_2026}, comprehensive real-world agent safety \\citep{vijayvargiya_openagentsafety_2026}, trajectory-level diagnosis \\citep{atbench_2026}, and deployment-condition probing \\citep{riskybench_2026}. \\citet{tamkin_evaluating_2023} evaluate how language models navigate policy-governed decisions, \\citet{shieldagent_2025} extract formal safety rules from policy documents, and AgentAuditor \\citep{agentauditor_2025} achieves human-level safety evaluation. Our work differs by focusing on the \\emph{source specification} rather than the agent's execution: we measure how ambiguity in the policy itself causes safety violations, complementing work that assumes clean policies.\n\n\\paragraph{Policy specification for agents.}\nDRIP-R \\citep{drip_r_2026} is the closest concurrent work: it benchmarks 400 policy-ambiguous retail scenarios across five RE ambiguity types, measuring decision quality rather than safety violations. Our work differs in three respects: (1) our matched-pair design establishes causality rather than correlating ambiguity presence with outcomes; (2) we measure safety violations rather than decision quality, enabling failure mechanism analysis; (3) our two-layer taxonomy adds linguistic-layer types, and we quantify the model$\\times$type interaction. We map DRIP-R's five RE ambiguity categories to our six-type taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:drip_mapping}. PolicyBank \\citep{policybank_2026} studies iterative policy refinement, closing 82\\% of the gap to a human oracle. AgentPex \\citep{agentpex_2026} detects procedural violations in agentic traces, finding that 83\\% of perfect-outcome traces contain violations. MANTRA \\citep{mantra_2026} synthesizes SMT-validated compliance benchmarks from procedural manuals. $\\tau$-bench \\citep{tau_bench_2024} and its successor $\\tau^2$-bench \\citep{tau2_bench_2025} provide the conversational agent evaluation platforms. ",

  "old_string": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. We present a matched-pair causal study on $\\tau^2$-bench with 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy, evaluated across five models (2,997 episodes). Ambiguous clauses are consistently nocuous (i.e., harmful), increasing violation rates by 34.0 percentage points (conditional OR $= 15.1$, matched-pair), with perturbation controls confirming specificity to semantic ambiguity. A variance decomposition reveals that individual clause wording explains 22.4\\% of violation variance versus 0.4\\% for ambiguity type and 1.0\\% for model identity, establishing clause-level wording as the dominant risk factor. However, a normalized mutual information analysis shows that type membership predicts failure \\emph{mechanism} 17$\\times$ more strongly than it predicts violation rate, establishing the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool for failure-mechanism prediction rather than risk prediction. Per-type effects form a partial ordering with robust extremes: incompleteness as the consistent upper bound ($+$49.2\\,pp, conditional on an information-addition confound) and coreferential as the lower bound ($+$18.7\\,pp), and a margin-dependent mid-tier structure spanning $+$30.8 to $+$36.0\\,pp. Error analysis of 614 violation traces reveals \\emph{Disambiguation Neglect} ($\\leq$11\\% per-model awareness) and \\emph{systematic nocuity}: 41.3\\% of clauses triggering model-convergent violations. These results, observed in customer-service policy domains, motivate clause-level policy auditing and type-targeted diagnosis for policy-governed deployments.\n\\end{abstract}",
  "new_string": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. We present a matched-pair causal study on $\\tau^2$-bench with 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy, evaluated across five models. Ambiguous clauses are consistently nocuous (i.e., harmful), increasing violation rates by 34.0 percentage points (conditional OR $= 15.1$, matched-pair), with perturbation controls confirming specificity to semantic ambiguity. A variance decomposition reveals that individual clause wording explains 22.4\\% of violation variance versus 0.4\\% for ambiguity type, establishing clause-level wording as the dominant risk factor. However, a normalized mutual information analysis shows that type membership predicts failure \\emph{mechanism} 17$\\times$ more strongly than it predicts violation rate, establishing the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool for failure-mechanism prediction rather than risk prediction. Per-type effects range from $+$18.7 to $+$49.2\\,pp, with incompleteness as the consistent upper bound. Error analysis reveals disambiguation neglect ($\\leq$11\\% per-model awareness) and \\emph{systematic nocuity}: 41.3\\% of clauses triggering model-convergent violations. These results, observed in customer-service policy domains, motivate clause-level policy auditing and type-targeted diagnosis for policy-governed deployments.\n\\end{abstract}"

We treat the two-layer structure as organizational convenience rather than a theoretical claim.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around four analyses of increasing specificity: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2), the aggregate layer effect (C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 constitute our primary claims; C3 is inconclusive; C4 is exploratory but provides the model$\\times$type interaction analysis.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity ($n = 300$, $\\Delta_{\\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\\,pp, CI containing zero). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\\footnote{Type III SS on binary outcomes approximates a linear probability model; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant factor ordering (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}).} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), model 1.0\\%, condition 15.8\\%, residual 60.8\\% (conversation trajectory, user-simulator stochasticity). A permutation test ($n = 1{,}000$, shuffling within type; Appendix~\\ref{app:clause_variance}) confirms the clause effect exceeds chance (null mean $= 8.3\\%$, $p < 0.001$); clause-level ICC $= 0.386$ (mixed-model $= 0.189$). Individual clause wording---not type membership---drives clause-level variance; the omnibus test on ambiguous episodes nonetheless confirms significant type heterogeneity ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $V = 0.137$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). Among confound-controlled types, per-type effects range from $+$18.7\\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\\,pp (authorization scope); incompleteness reaches $+$49.2\\,pp but is conditional on an information-addition confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). A replication on two additional model families (Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}) confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp, both $p < 10^{-6}$); excluding incompleteness, it remains large ($\\Delta = 31.0$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sans_incompleteness}). A cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency across all five models ($\\Delta = +13.8$\\,pp pooled; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy's primary value lies not in predicting clause risk but in predicting failure mechanisms: type predicts mechanism 17$\\times$ more strongly than violation rate (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Tier: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Tier & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). TOST equivalence testing at a $\\pm 15$ pp margin reveals a graded equivalence structure among five types---authorization scope (41.6\\%), lexical (42.8\\%), scopal (39.4\\%), and conditional precedence (36.8\\%) form a mid-tier cluster, with coreferential (31.6\\%) below---while incompleteness (53.6\\%) stands apart as an upper bound---conditional on the confound controls in Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}---whose ranking partly reflects an information-addition confound. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nSensitivity analysis across seven margins ($\\pm 5$--$20$ pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}) confirms this structure is margin-dependent: mid-tier cohesion emerges at $\\pm 12$ pp and stabilizes at $\\pm 15$ pp, while the incompleteness upper-bound is robust across all margins. A bootstrap cluster analysis ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) corroborates: incompleteness forms a singleton at $k = 2$, coreferential additionally separates at $k = 3$. Cross-model type ranking concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}), driven by incompleteness ranking highest in four of five models.\n\nAn important qualification: cross-judge-group type rankings are discordant ($\\rho = 0.09$, $p = 0.87$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), driven by authorization scope ranking first in one judge group but last in the other. Excluding it, the remaining five types show strong concordance ($\\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$). C2 should be interpreted as establishing robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound---rather than a complete linear ordering.\n\nIncompleteness is highest in four of five models (rates $\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}). Its unambiguous variant adds policy content, so the 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound; a confound analysis confirms significance after controlling for information delta (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). Coreferential, whose variants are lexically identical, provides a confound-free lower bound at 31.6\\%.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Layer Effect (C3)}\n\nSpecification-layer types trend higher than linguistic-layer types (44.0\\% vs.\\ 37.9\\%, $p = 0.017$), but C3 is inconclusive: the effect does not survive Holm--Bonferroni correction, controlling for token delta reverses the direction, and cross-layer type overlap (conditional precedence 36.8\\% vs.\\ scopal 39.4\\%) further undermines the distinction. Type-level distinctions (C2) hold independently of layer grouping. We accordingly treat the two-layer structure as organizational convenience rather than a theoretical claim.\n\n\\subsection{Model $\\times$ Type Interaction (C4) \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:c4}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) by model and type under ambiguous conditions ($n = 2{,}997$). Authorization scope varies 52\\,pp across models ($\\kappa = 0.385$); incompleteness remains uniformly high (46--62\\%). C4 interaction is authorization-scope-specific (excluding it: $p = 0.97$).}\n\\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nA model main effect is significant ($\\chi^2 = 31.02$, $p = 3.0 \\times 10^{-6}$, $V = 0.102$), with Claude (30.3\\%) substantially less vulnerable than DeepSeek (49.7\\%). Model identity explains only 1.0\\% of total violation variance; the significant interaction ($F(20, 2976) = 1.97$, $p = 0.006$; $\\mathrm{df} = (5{-}1) \\times (6{-}1) = 20$; 2.9\\% of variance; confirmed by logistic regression likelihood ratio test: $\\chi^2(20) = 47.20$, $p = 5.5 \\times 10^{-4}$) concentrates in specific type--model combinations.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:heatmap} highlights three patterns: (1) authorization scope is the \\emph{most model-differentiating type} (52\\,pp range; Claude 12\\% to GPT-5.4 64\\%), though its low judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$) adds uncertainty; (2) incompleteness is a \\emph{consistent vulnerability} (46--62\\%); (3) conditional precedence is the most \\emph{model-invariant} (34--40\\%). The authorization scope ranking is stable within the GPT-5.4 judge subset (bootstrap preservation: 99.5\\%), and the 34\\,pp within-judge range far exceeds measurement noise; given the low cross-judge $\\kappa$, we characterize this as suggestive (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}). Excluding authorization scope, the residual interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}).\n\n",

    "oldString": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. We present a matched-pair causal study on $\\tau^2$-bench with 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy, evaluated across five models (2,997 episodes). Ambiguous clauses are consistently nocuous (i.e., harmful), increasing violation rates by 34.0 percentage points (conditional OR $= 15.1$, matched-pair), with perturbation controls confirming specificity to semantic ambiguity. A variance decomposition reveals that individual clause wording explains 22.4\\% of violation variance versus 0.4\\% for ambiguity type and 1.0\\% for model identity, establishing clause-level wording as the dominant risk factor. However, a normalized mutual information analysis shows that type membership predicts failure \\emph{mechanism} 17$\\times$ more strongly than it predicts violation rate, establishing the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool for failure-mechanism prediction rather than risk prediction. Per-type effects form a partial ordering with robust extremes: incompleteness as the consistent upper bound ($+$49.2\\,pp, conditional on an information-addition confound) and coreferential as the lower bound ($+$18.7\\,pp), and a margin-dependent mid-tier structure spanning $+$30.8 to $+$36.0\\,pp. Error analysis of 614 violation traces reveals \\emph{Disambiguation Neglect} ($\\leq$11\\% per-model awareness) and \\emph{systematic nocuity}: 41.3\\% of clauses triggering model-convergent violations.     "oldString": "Our contributions are: (1)~the first matched-pair causal study of policy ambiguity's safety impact with perturbation control (\\S\\ref{sec:method}), observed across five primary models and confirmed in two additional replication models; (2)~a variance decomposition showing that individual clause wording (22.4\\%) dominates type membership (0.4\\%) and model identity (1.0\\%), combined with a mutual information analysis demonstrating that type predicts failure mechanism 17$\\times$ better than violation rate (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}, \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), repositioning the taxonomy from a risk-prediction tool to a diagnostic taxonomy for failure-mechanism prediction; and (3)~an error analysis quantifying \\emph{Disambiguation Neglect} in consequential agentic settings---extending LLM ambiguity-collapse findings \\citep{liu_ambiguity_2023,hu_ambiguity_2025} to agentic settings---with $\\leq$11\\% per-model awareness (4.6\\% overall), and \\emph{systematic nocuity}: 41.3\\% of ambiguous clauses trigger majority-convergent violations, indicating clause-level risk factors (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).",
    "newString": "Our contributions are: (1)~the first matched-pair causal study of policy ambiguity's safety impact with perturbation control (\\S\\ref{sec:method}), observed across five primary models and confirmed in two additional replication models; (2)~a variance decomposition revealing that clause wording explains 22.4\\% of violation variance versus 0.4\\% for type, combined with a mutual information analysis showing that type predicts failure mechanism 17$\\times$ better than violation rate, motivating type-level analysis as a diagnostic rather than predictive tool (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}, \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}); and (3)~an error analysis revealing type-specific failure mechanisms and convergent violation patterns across models (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nA customer asks to return a customized item within the 30-day window. The agent consults the store policy, which states that ``items may be returned within 30 days.'' The word ``items'' scopes over all product categories, including custom orders that are normally final sale. The agent grants a full refund and arranges free return shipping. One word of scopal ambiguity costs the retailer \\$200.\n\nLLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that govern their permitted actions. These specifications inevitably contain ambiguity: a property that has been studied for decades in requirements engineering (RE) and computational linguistics. \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004} distinguish between \\emph{nocuous} (i.e., harmful) ambiguity, where different stakeholders arrive at different interpretations, and \\emph{innocuous} ambiguity, where shared organizational context ensures convergent interpretation. In human RE workflows, most ambiguity is innocuous because developers share domain knowledge, can consult colleagues, and iteratively clarify requirements.\n\nLLM agents lack these disambiguation channels. They cannot consult colleagues, rarely escalate to users, and must resolve ambiguity in real time from the text alone. This raises a question that has not been empirically addressed: does policy ambiguity cause safety violations in LLM agents, and if so, does the \\emph{type} of ambiguity predict the severity of the violation? Prior work classifies ambiguity types in policies \\citep{drip_r_2026,li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} or measures policy understanding quality \\citep{policybank_2026}, but no existing study provides a causal, type-granularity mapping from ambiguity to safety violations.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Experimental overview. We construct 300 matched clause pairs across a six-type, two-layer ambiguity taxonomy and inject them into $\\tau^2$-bench policy documents. Five models interact with the modified policies across 2,997 episodes. A cross-judge evaluation pipeline classifies violation severity on a four-tier scale.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe address this gap with a matched-pair causal study on $\\tau^2$-bench \\citep{tau2_bench_2025}, a dual-control conversational agent benchmark. Using a six-type taxonomy organized into two layers---linguistic and specification, including specification-level gaps that produce equivalent behavioral effects in LLM agents (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}), we construct 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs and evaluate five architecturally diverse models across 2,997 episodes.\n\nOur results show that policy ambiguity is consistently nocuous for LLM agents ($+34.0$ pp violation rate increase, conditional OR $= 15.1$), but risk is driven by individual clause wording (22.4\\% of variance) rather than type membership (0.4\\%). The taxonomy's value lies in predicting failure mechanisms: type membership predicts the dominant mechanism 17$\\times$ better than it predicts violation rate ($\\text{NMI} = 0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), enabling targeted diagnosis (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}). Incompleteness is consistently the highest-risk type (46--62\\% across all models); the remaining types form a partial ordering with robust extremes but margin-dependent mid-tier structure.\n\nOur contributions are: (1)~the first matched-pair causal study of policy ambiguity's safety impact with perturbation control (\\S\\ref{sec:method}), observed across five primary models and confirmed in two additional replication models; (2)~a variance decomposition showing that individual clause wording (22.4\\%) dominates type membership (0.4\\%) and model identity (1.0\\%), combined with a mutual information analysis demonstrating that type predicts failure mechanism 17$\\times$ better than violation rate (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}, \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), repositioning the taxonomy from a risk-prediction tool to a diagnostic taxonomy for failure-mechanism prediction; and (3)~an error analysis quantifying \\emph{Disambiguation Neglect} in consequential agentic settings---extending LLM ambiguity-collapse findings \\citep{liu_ambiguity_2023,hu_ambiguity_2025} to agentic settings---with $\\leq$11\\% per-model awareness (4.6\\% overall), and \\emph{systematic nocuity}: 41.3\\% of ambiguous clauses trigger majority-convergent violations, indicating clause-level risk factors (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n",

Our taxonomy serves as an experimental instrument for controlled causal analysis rather than a proposed classification standard.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Ambiguity in NLP and requirements engineering.}\nAmbiguity has been studied extensively in both computational linguistics and software engineering. Word sense disambiguation \\citep{navigli_wsd_2009} addresses lexical ambiguity at the token level; our lexical type extends this to policy-specific polysemy. \\citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} propose an 11-type NLP taxonomy covering syntactic, semantic, and pragmatic ambiguity, from which we draw our linguistic-layer types. \\citet{kamath_scope_2024} investigate scopal ambiguity in LLMs, finding systematic preferences for surface scope. In requirements engineering (RE), \\citet{massey_ambiguity_re_2014} classify six ambiguity types in regulatory texts, and \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004} introduce the distinction between nocuous ambiguity (where stakeholders diverge on interpretation) and innocuous ambiguity (where they converge). Computational detection of nocuous ambiguity has progressed from heuristic methods \\citep{chantree_2006,willis_nocuous_2008} through NLP-based cross-domain detectors \\citep{ferrari_ambiguity_2019,ferrari_defects_2018} to transformer-based approaches for anaphoric ambiguity \\citep{ezzini_taphsir_2022,ezzini_anaphoric_2022}. \\citet{zhao_nlp4re_2021} survey the broader NLP4RE landscape. Our work extends Berry and Kamsties' framework to LLM agents, where the absence of shared organizational context makes previously innocuous ambiguity nocuous.\n\n\\paragraph{Psycholinguistics of ambiguity processing.}\n\\citet{ferreira_good_enough_2002} show that human comprehenders build shallow, ``good-enough'' representations rather than full parses, paralleling our observation that LLM agents adopt surface interpretations. \\citet{frazier_rayner_1982} demonstrate early commitment to a single parse; \\citet{li_garden_path_llm_2024} provide direct evidence that LLMs exhibit analogous garden-path effects. At the pragmatic level, \\citet{hu_pragmatic_2023} and \\citet{ruis_goldilocks_2023} show that LLMs struggle with implicature resolution, defaulting to literal interpretations even when pragmatic inference is required. \\citet{liu_ambiguity_2023} demonstrate that language models fail to represent ambiguity, collapsing multiple interpretations into a single output; \\citet{gur_arieh_ambiguity_collapse_2026} taxonomize the epistemic risks of this ``ambiguity collapse''; \\citet{sedova_to_know_2024} show analogous self-inconsistencies under entity-type ambiguity. These findings ground our \\emph{Disambiguation Neglect} result: agents process ambiguous input without meta-cognitive awareness but, unlike humans, lack social channels to recover. \\citet{disambiguation_cqa_survey_2025} survey disambiguation strategies in conversational QA; \\citet{suri_structured_2025} show that structured uncertainty estimation improves coverage by 7--39\\%, while \\citet{sehwag_aspi_2026} demonstrate that clarification-seeking introduces prompt injection vulnerabilities---a tension for disambiguation channel design.\n\n\\paragraph{Agent safety evaluation.}\nThe agent safety evaluation landscape includes benchmarks for risk awareness \\citep{rjudge_2024}, tool-use safety \\citep{toolemu_2024}, indirect prompt injection \\citep{injecagent_2024}, safe task planning \\citep{yin_safeagentbench_2025}, policy-invisible violations \\citep{phantompolicy_2026}, comprehensive real-world agent safety \\citep{vijayvargiya_openagentsafety_2026}, trajectory-level diagnosis \\citep{atbench_2026}, and deployment-condition probing \\citep{riskybench_2026}. \\citet{tamkin_evaluating_2023} evaluate how language models navigate policy-governed decisions, \\citet{shieldagent_2025} extract formal safety rules from policy documents, and AgentAuditor \\citep{agentauditor_2025} achieves human-level safety evaluation. Our work differs by focusing on the \\emph{source specification} rather than the agent's execution: we measure how ambiguity in the policy itself causes safety violations, complementing work that assumes clean policies.\n\n\\paragraph{Policy specification for agents.}\nDRIP-R \\citep{drip_r_2026} is the closest concurrent work: it benchmarks 400 policy-ambiguous retail scenarios across five RE ambiguity types, measuring decision quality rather than safety violations. Our work differs in three respects: (1) our matched-pair design establishes causality rather than correlating ambiguity presence with outcomes; (2) we measure safety violations rather than decision quality, enabling failure mechanism analysis; (3) our two-layer taxonomy adds linguistic-layer types, and we quantify the model$\\times$type interaction. We map DRIP-R's five RE ambiguity categories to our six-type taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:drip_mapping}. PolicyBank \\citep{policybank_2026} studies iterative policy refinement, closing 82\\% of the gap to a human oracle. AgentPex \\citep{agentpex_2026} detects procedural violations in agentic traces, finding that 83\\% of perfect-outcome traces contain violations. MANTRA \\citep{mantra_2026} synthesizes SMT-validated compliance benchmarks from procedural manuals. $\\tau$-bench \\citep{tau_bench_2024} and its successor $\\tau^2$-bench \\citep{tau2_bench_2025} provide the conversational agent evaluation platforms. ",

All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity ($n = 300$, $\\Delta_{\\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\\,pp, CI containing zero). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\\footnote{Type III SS on binary outcomes approximates a linear probability model; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant factor ordering (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}), though magnitudes differ between linear and logistic specifications.} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), model 1.0\\%, condition 15.8\\%, residual 60.8\\% (conversation trajectory, user-simulator stochasticity).     "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around four analyses of increasing specificity: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2), the aggregate layer effect (C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 constitute our primary claims; C3 is inconclusive; C4 is exploratory but provides the model$\\times$type interaction analysis.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). The taxonomy's primary value lies not in predicting clause risk but in predicting failure mechanisms: type predicts mechanism 17$\\times$ more strongly than violation rate (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Tier: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Tier & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). TOST equivalence testing at a $\\pm 15$ pp margin reveals a graded equivalence structure among five types---authorization scope (41.6\\%), lexical (42.8\\%), scopal (39.4\\%), and conditional precedence (36.8\\%) form a mid-tier cluster, with coreferential (31.6\\%) below---while incompleteness (53.6\\%) stands apart as an upper bound---conditional on the confound controls in Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}---whose ranking partly reflects an information-addition confound. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nSensitivity analysis across seven margins ($\\pm 5$--$20$ pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}) confirms this structure is margin-dependent: mid-tier cohesion emerges at $\\pm 12$ pp and stabilizes at $\\pm 15$ pp, while the incompleteness upper-bound is robust across all margins. A bootstrap cluster analysis ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) corroborates: incompleteness forms a singleton at $k = 2$, coreferential additionally separates at $k = 3$. Cross-model type ranking concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}), driven by incompleteness ranking highest in four of five models.\n\nAn important qualification: cross-judge-group type rankings are discordant ($\\rho = 0.09$, $p = 0.87$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), driven by authorization scope ranking first in one judge group but last in the other. Excluding it, the remaining five types show strong concordance ($\\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$). C2 should be interpreted as establishing robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound---rather than a complete linear ordering.\n\nIncompleteness is highest in four of five models (rates $\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}). Its unambiguous variant adds policy content, so the 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound; a confound analysis confirms significance after controlling for information delta (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). Coreferential, whose variants are lexically identical, provides a confound-free lower bound at 31.6\\%.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Layer Effect (C3)}\n\nSpecification-layer types trend higher than linguistic-layer types (44.0\\% vs.\\ 37.9\\%, $p = 0.017$), but C3 is inconclusive: the effect does not survive Holm--Bonferroni correction and controlling for token delta reverses the direction. We treat the two-layer structure as organizational convenience rather than a theoretical claim.\n\n\\subsection{Model $\\times$ Type Interaction (C4) \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:c4}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) by model and type under ambiguous conditions ($n = 2{,}997$). Authorization scope varies 52\\,pp across models ($\\kappa = 0.385$); incompleteness remains uniformly high (46--62\\%). C4 interaction is authorization-scope-specific (excluding it: $p = 0.97$).}\n\\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nA model main effect is significant ($\\chi^2 = 31.02$, $p = 3.0 \\times 10^{-6}$, $V = 0.102$), with Claude (30.3\\%) substantially less vulnerable than DeepSeek (49.7\\%). Model identity explains only 1.0\\% of total violation variance; the significant interaction ($F(20, 2976) = 1.97$, $p = 0.006$; $\\mathrm{df} = (5{-}1) \\times (6{-}1) = 20$; 2.9\\% of variance; confirmed by logistic regression likelihood ratio test: $\\chi^2(20) = 47.20$, $p = 5.5 \\times 10^{-4}$) concentrates in specific type--model combinations.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:heatmap} highlights three patterns: (1) authorization scope is the \\emph{most model-differentiating type} (52\\,pp range; Claude 12\\% to GPT-5.4 64\\%), though its low judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$) adds uncertainty; (2) incompleteness is a \\emph{consistent vulnerability} (46--62\\%); (3) conditional precedence is the most \\emph{model-invariant} (34--40\\%). The authorization scope ranking is stable within the GPT-5.4 judge subset (bootstrap preservation: 99.5\\%), and the 34\\,pp within-judge range far exceeds measurement noise; given the low cross-judge $\\kappa$, we characterize this as suggestive (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}). Excluding authorization scope, the residual interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}).\n\n",

A permutation test ($n = 1{,}000$, shuffling within type; Appendix~\\ref{app:clause_variance}) confirms the clause effect exceeds chance (null mean $= 8.3\\%$, $p < 0.001$); clause-level ICC $= 0.386$ (mixed-model $= 0.189$). Individual clause wording---not type membership---drives clause-level variance; the omnibus test on ambiguous episodes nonetheless confirms significant type heterogeneity ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $V = 0.137$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). Among confound-controlled types, per-type effects range from $+$18.7\\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\\,pp (authorization scope); incompleteness reaches $+$49.2\\,pp but is conditional on an information-addition confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). A replication on two additional model families (Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}) confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp, both $p < 10^{-6}$); excluding incompleteness, it remains large ($\\Delta = 31.0$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sans_incompleteness}). A cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency across all five models ($\\Delta = +13.8$\\,pp pooled; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}).
