```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around four analyses of increasing specificity: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2), the aggregate layer effect (C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 constitute our primary claims; C3 is inconclusive; C4 is exploratory but provides the model$\\times$type interaction analysis.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity ($n = 300$, $\\Delta_{\\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\\,pp, CI containing zero). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\\footnote{Type III SS on binary outcomes approximates a linear probability model; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant factor ordering (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}), though magnitudes differ between linear and logistic specifications.} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), model 1.0\\%, condition 15.8\\%, residual 60.8\\% (conversation trajectory, user-simulator stochasticity). A permutation test ($n = 1{,}000$, shuffling within type; Appendix~\\ref{app:clause_variance}) confirms the clause effect exceeds chance (null mean $= 8.3\\%$, $p < 0.001$); clause-level ICC $= 0.386$ under ambiguous conditions (full-data ICC $= 0.189$). Individual clause wording---not type membership---drives clause-level variance; the omnibus test on ambiguous episodes nonetheless confirms significant type heterogeneity ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $V = 0.137$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\\,pp) is an upper bound that includes information addition; the confound-controlled range is $+$18.7\\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\\,pp (authorization scope; Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}).\n\nReplication on two additional model families confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp, both $p < 10^{-6}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); a cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency across all five models ($+13.8$\\,pp pooled; three individually significant at $p < 0.05$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy's primary value lies not in predicting clause risk but in predicting failure mechanisms: type predicts mechanism 17$\\times$ more strongly than violation rate (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Tier: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Tier & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). TOST equivalence testing at a $\\pm 15$ pp margin reveals a graded equivalence structure among five types---authorization scope (41.6\\%), lexical (42.8\\%), scopal (39.4\\%), and conditional precedence (36.8\\%) form a mid-tier cluster, with coreferential (31.6\\%) below---while incompleteness (53.6\\%) stands apart as an upper bound---conditional on the confound controls in Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}---whose ranking partly reflects an information-addition confound. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nSensitivity analysis across seven margins ($\\pm 5$--$20$ pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}) confirms this structure is margin-dependent: mid-tier cohesion emerges at $\\pm 12$ pp and stabilizes at $\\pm 15$ pp, while the incompleteness upper-bound is robust across all margins. A bootstrap cluster analysis ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) corroborates: incompleteness forms a singleton at $k = 2$, coreferential additionally separates at $k = 3$. Cross-model type ranking concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}), driven by incompleteness ranking highest in four of five models.\n\nAn important qualification: cross-judge-group type rankings are discordant ($\\rho = 0.09$, $p = 0.87$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), driven by authorization scope ranking first in one judge group but last in the other. Excluding it, the remaining five types show strong concordance ($\\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$). C2 should be interpreted as establishing robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound---rather than a complete linear ordering.\n\nIncompleteness is highest in four of five models (rates $\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}). Its unambiguous variant adds policy content, so the 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound; a confound analysis confirms significance after controlling for information delta (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). Coreferential, whose variants are lexically identical, provides a confound-free lower bound at 31.6\\%.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Layer Effect (C3)}\n\nSpecification-layer types trend higher than linguistic-layer types (44.0\\% vs.\\ 37.9\\%, $p = 0.017$), but C3 is inconclusive: the effect does not survive Holm--Bonferroni correction and controlling for token delta reverses the direction. We treat the two-layer structure as organizational convenience rather than a theoretical claim.\n\n\\subsection{Model $\\times$ Type Interaction (C4) \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:c4}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) by model and type under ambiguous conditions ($n = 2{,}997$). Authorization scope varies 52\\,pp across models ($\\kappa = 0.385$); incompleteness remains uniformly high (46--62\\%). C4 interaction is authorization-scope-specific (excluding it: $p = 0.97$).}\n\\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nA model main effect is significant ($\\chi^2 = 31.02$, $p = 3.0 \\times 10^{-6}$, $V = 0.102$), with Claude (30.3\\%) substantially less vulnerable than DeepSeek (49.7\\%). Model identity explains only 1.0\\% of total violation variance; the significant interaction ($F(20, 2976) = 1.97$, $p = 0.006$; $\\mathrm{df} = (5{-}1) \\times (6{-}1) = 20$; 2.9\\% of variance; confirmed by logistic regression likelihood ratio test: $\\chi^2(20) = 47.20$, $p = 5.5 \\times 10^{-4}$) concentrates in specific type--model combinations.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:heatmap} highlights three patterns: (1) authorization scope is the \\emph{most model-differentiating type} (52\\,pp range; Claude 12\\% to GPT-5.4 64\\%), though its low judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$) adds uncertainty; (2) incompleteness is a \\emph{consistent vulnerability} (46--62\\%); (3) conditional precedence is the most \\emph{model-invariant} (34--40\\%). The authorization scope ranking is stable within the GPT-5.4 judge subset (bootstrap preservation: 99.5\\%), and the 34\\,pp within-judge range far exceeds measurement noise; given the low cross-judge $\\kappa$, we characterize this as suggestive (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}). Excluding authorization scope, the residual interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}).\n\n",
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around four analyses of increasing specificity: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2), the aggregate layer effect (C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 constitute our primary claims; C3 is inconclusive; C4 is exploratory but provides the model$\\times$type interaction analysis.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity ($n = 300$, $\\Delta_{\\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\\,pp, CI containing zero). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\\footnote{Type III SS on binary outcomes approximates a linear probability model; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant factor ordering (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}), though magnitudes differ between linear and logistic specifications.} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), model 1.0\\%, condition 15.8\\%, residual 60.8\\% (conversation trajectory, user-simulator stochasticity). A permutation test ($n = 1{,}000$, shuffling within type; Appendix~\\ref{app:clause_variance}) confirms the clause effect exceeds chance (null mean $= 8.3\\%$, $p < 0.001$); clause-level ICC $= 0.386$ under ambiguous conditions (full-data ICC $= 0.189$). Individual clause wording---not type membership---drives clause-level variance; the omnibus test on ambiguous episodes nonetheless confirms significant type heterogeneity ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $V = 0.137$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\\,pp) is an upper bound that includes information addition; the confound-controlled range is $+$18.7\\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\\,pp (authorization scope; Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}).\n\nReplication on two additional model families confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp, both $p < 10^{-6}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); a cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency across all five models ($+13.8$\\,pp pooled; three individually significant at $p < 0.05$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy's primary value lies not in predicting clause risk but in predicting failure mechanisms: type predicts mechanism 17$\\times$ more strongly than violation rate (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Tier: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Tier & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). TOST equivalence testing at a $\\pm 15$ pp margin reveals a graded equivalence structure among five types---authorization scope (41.6\\%), lexical (42.8\\%), scopal (39.4\\%), and conditional precedence (36.8\\%) form a mid-tier cluster, with coreferential (31.6\\%) below---while incompleteness (53.6\\%) stands apart as an upper bound---conditional on the confound controls in Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}---whose ranking partly reflects an information-addition confound. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nSensitivity analysis across seven margins ($\\pm 5$--$20$ pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}) confirms this structure is margin-dependent: mid-tier cohesion emerges at $\\pm 12$ pp and stabilizes at $\\pm 15$ pp, while the incompleteness upper-bound is robust across all margins. A bootstrap cluster analysis ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) corroborates: incompleteness forms a singleton at $k = 2$, coreferential additionally separates at $k = 3$. Cross-model type ranking concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}), driven by incompleteness ranking highest in four of five models.\n\nAn important qualification: cross-judge-group type rankings are discordant ($\\rho = 0.09$, $p = 0.87$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), driven by authorization scope ranking first in one judge group but last in the other. Excluding it, the remaining five types show strong concordance ($\\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$). C2 should be interpreted as establishing robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound---rather than a complete linear ordering.\n\nIncompleteness is highest in four of five models (rates $\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}). Its unambiguous variant adds policy content, so the 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound; a confound analysis confirms significance after controlling for information delta (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). Coreferential, whose variants are lexically identical, provides a confound-free lower bound at 31.6\\%.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Layer Effect (C3)}\n\nSpecification-layer types trend higher than linguistic-layer types (44.0\\% vs.\\ 37.9\\%, $p = 0.017$), but C3 is inconclusive: the effect does not survive Holm--Bonferroni correction and controlling for token delta reverses the direction. We treat the two-layer structure as organizational convenience rather than a theoretical claim.\n\n\\subsection{Model $\\times$ Type Interaction (C4) \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:c4}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) by model and type under ambiguous conditions ($n = 2{,}997$). Authorization scope varies 52\\,pp across models ($\\kappa = 0.385$); incompleteness remains uniformly high (46--62\\%). C4 interaction is authorization-scope-specific (excluding it: $p = 0.97$).}\n\\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nA model main effect is significant ($\\chi^2 = 31.02$, $p = 3.0 \\times 10^{-6}$, $V = 0.102$), with Claude (30.3\\%) substantially less vulnerable than DeepSeek (49.7\\%). Model identity explains only 1.0\\% of total violation variance; the significant interaction ($F(20, 2976) = 1.97$, $p = 0.006$; $\\mathrm{df} = (5{-}1) \\times (6{-}1) = 20$; 2.9\\% of variance; confirmed by logistic regression likelihood ratio test: $\\chi^2(20) = 47.20$, $p = 5.5 \\times 10^{-4}$) concentrates in specific type--model combinations.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:heatmap} highlights three patterns: (1) authorization scope is the \\emph{most model-differentiating type} (52\\,pp range; Claude 12\\% to GPT-5.4 64\\%), though its low judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$) adds uncertainty; (2) incompleteness is a \\emph{consistent vulnerability} (46--62\\%); (3) conditional precedence is the most \\emph{model-invariant} (34--40\\%). The authorization scope ranking is stable within the GPT-5.4 judge subset (bootstrap preservation: 99.5\\%), and the 34\\,pp within-judge range far exceeds measurement noise; given the low cross-judge $\\kappa$, we characterize this as suggestive given the single-judge-per-model design (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}). Excluding authorization scope, the residual interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}).\n\n",
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Error Analysis \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\nWe analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes (Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$, $\\kappa = 0.52$ \\citep{gwet_2014,cohen_1960}). Using LLMs to code LLM failures introduces circularity risk; however, mutual information between type (exogenous) and mechanism labels ($I(\\text{type}; \\text{mechanism}) = 0.41$ bits, $V = 0.45$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}) provides structural evidence against this.\n\n\\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}\n\nThe five-model data reveal three mechanistically distinct failure patterns that crosscut the specification/linguistic layer boundary.\n\n\\emph{Structural misparse}: the agent constructs an incorrect parse and follows it consistently, paralleling garden-path processing \\citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002} at a behavioral level. Dominates scopal (5/5 models), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Information gap-filling}: the agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults. Dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Boundary violation}: the agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified; 69.2\\% involve unauthorized escalation with zero conservative refusals ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial). Dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).\n\nEach type's dominant failure mode is majority-consistent ($\\geq$3/5 models), with two achieving unanimity (scopal and authorization scope).\n\n\\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}\n\nAgents rarely recognize ambiguity. We code \\emph{disambiguation awareness} versus \\emph{surface adoption} (proceeding with a single interpretation). Overall awareness is 4.6\\% ($\\leq$11\\% per model); Qwen3 and DeepSeek show zero across all 278 combined traces.\n\nWe term this \\emph{Disambiguation Neglect}---the absence of disambiguation in default settings, not inability. Prior work shows LLMs collapse ambiguity into single interpretations \\citep{liu_ambiguity_2023,hu_ambiguity_2025}; we extend this to agentic contexts where the result is irreversible policy violation. Our mitigation experiment ($-$8.5\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms disambiguation can be elicited.\n\n\\subsection{Systematic Nocuity}\n\nAmong ambiguous clauses, 41.3\\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\\geq$3/5 models), degrading monotonically across thresholds ($\\geq$2/5: 54.7\\%, $\\geq$4/5: 23.7\\%, 5/5: 13.0\\%)---a phenomenon we term \\emph{systematic nocuity} \\citep{berry_kamsties_2004}: models converge on the same incorrect interpretation, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. Of these, 83.9\\% share the same dominant failure mode, confirming convergent---not coincident---violations. Incompleteness has the highest universal rate (24\\%) and is disproportionately represented (OR $= 2.61$, $p = 0.019$).\n",
```

```json
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Differential nocuity.}\nBuilding on \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}'s nocuous/innocuous distinction, our results support a \\emph{differential nocuity} hypothesis \\citep{willis_nocuous_2008}: risk depends on type, safety training, and disambiguation channel availability. The binary effect is domain-robust (interaction $p = 0.187$) but type rankings are not ($\\rho = 0.26$), suggesting nocuity is universal while its type-specific profile requires per-domain calibration.\n\n\\paragraph{Taxonomy as diagnostic tool.}\nType membership explains only 0.4\\% of violation variance versus 22.4\\% for clause wording; however, it predicts failure \\emph{mechanism} 17$\\times$ more strongly than violation rate ($\\text{NMI}$ ratio $= 16.8$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), validating the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Differential nocuity.}\nBuilding on \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}'s nocuous/innocuous distinction, our results support a \\emph{differential nocuity} hypothesis \\citep{willis_nocuous_2008}: risk depends on type, safety training, and disambiguation channel availability. The binary effect is domain-robust (interaction $p = 0.187$) but type rankings are not ($\\rho = 0.26$), suggesting nocuity is universal while its type-specific profile requires per-domain calibration. Nevertheless, 60.8\\% of violation variance remains unexplained, reflecting conversation trajectory and user-simulator stochasticity; clause-level auditing addresses the largest controllable factor (22.4\\%) but does not eliminate deployment-time randomness.\n\n\\paragraph{Taxonomy as diagnostic tool.}\nType membership explains only 0.4\\% of violation variance versus 22.4\\% for clause wording; however, it predicts failure \\emph{mechanism} 17$\\times$ more strongly than violation rate (NMI ratio $\\approx 17$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), validating the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis})."
```

```json
    "newString": "\\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}\n\\label{sec:dn}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Error Analysis \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\nWe analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes (Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$, $\\kappa = 0.52$ \\citep{gwet_2014,cohen_1960}). Using LLMs to code LLM failures introduces circularity risk; however, mutual information between type (exogenous) and mechanism labels ($I(\\text{type}; \\text{mechanism}) = 0.41$ bits, $V = 0.45$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}) provides structural evidence against this.\n\n\\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}\n\nThe five-model data reveal three mechanistically distinct failure patterns that crosscut the specification/linguistic layer boundary.\n\n\\emph{Structural misparse}: the agent constructs an incorrect parse and follows it consistently, paralleling garden-path processing \\citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002} at a behavioral level. Dominates scopal (5/5 models), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Information gap-filling}: the agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults. Dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Boundary violation}: the agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified; 69.2\\% involve unauthorized escalation with zero conservative refusals ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial). Dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).\n\nEach type's dominant failure mode is majority-consistent ($\\geq$3/5 models), with two achieving unanimity (scopal and authorization scope).\n\n\\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}\n\nAgents rarely recognize ambiguity. We code \\emph{disambiguation awareness} versus \\emph{surface adoption} (proceeding with a single interpretation). Overall awareness is 4.6\\% ($\\leq$11\\% per model); Qwen3 and DeepSeek show zero across all 278 combined traces.\n\nWe refer to this pattern as \\emph{disambiguation neglect}---the absence of disambiguation in default settings, not inability. Prior work shows LLMs collapse ambiguity into single interpretations \\citep{liu_ambiguity_2023,hu_ambiguity_2025}; we extend this to agentic contexts where the result is irreversible policy violation. Our mitigation experiment ($-$8.5\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms disambiguation can be elicited.\n\n\\subsection{Systematic Nocuity}\n\nAmong ambiguous clauses, 41.3\\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\\geq$3/5 models), degrading monotonically across thresholds ($\\geq$2/5: 54.7\\%, $\\geq$4/5: 23.7\\%, 5/5: 13.0\\%)---a phenomenon we term \\emph{systematic nocuity} \\citep{berry_kamsties_2004}: models converge on the same incorrect interpretation, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. Of these, 83.9\\% share the same dominant failure mode, confirming convergent---not coincident---violations. Incompleteness has the highest universal rate (24\\%) and is disproportionately represented (OR $= 2.61$, $p = 0.019$).\n",
```

```json
  "parentUuid": "c449da6b-32b9-43ab-9eb1-63bceace75d4",

    "oldString": "These findings ground our \\emph{Disambiguation Neglect} result:",
    "newString": "These findings ground our \\emph{disambiguation neglect} result:",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Ambiguity in NLP and requirements engineering.}\nAmbiguity has been studied extensively in both computational linguistics and software engineering. Word sense disambiguation \\citep{navigli_wsd_2009} addresses lexical ambiguity at the token level; our lexical type extends this to policy-specific polysemy. \\citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} propose an 11-type NLP taxonomy covering syntactic, semantic, and pragmatic ambiguity, from which we draw our linguistic-layer types. \\citet{kamath_scope_2024} investigate scopal ambiguity in LLMs, finding systematic preferences for surface scope. In requirements engineering (RE), \\citet{massey_ambiguity_re_2014} classify six ambiguity types in regulatory texts, and \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004} introduce the distinction between nocuous ambiguity (where stakeholders diverge on interpretation) and innocuous ambiguity (where they converge). Computational detection of nocuous ambiguity has progressed from heuristic methods \\citep{chantree_2006,willis_nocuous_2008} through NLP-based cross-domain detectors \\citep{ferrari_ambiguity_2019,ferrari_defects_2018} to transformer-based approaches for anaphoric ambiguity \\citep{ezzini_taphsir_2022,ezzini_anaphoric_2022}. \\citet{zhao_nlp4re_2021} survey the broader NLP4RE landscape. Our work extends Berry and Kamsties' framework to LLM agents, where the absence of shared organizational context makes previously innocuous ambiguity nocuous. Our taxonomy serves as an experimental instrument for controlled causal analysis rather than a proposed classification standard.\n\n\\paragraph{Psycholinguistics of ambiguity processing.}\n\\citet{ferreira_good_enough_2002} show that human comprehenders build shallow, ``good-enough'' representations rather than full parses, paralleling our observation that LLM agents adopt surface interpretations. \\citet{frazier_rayner_1982} demonstrate early commitment to a single parse; \\citet{li_garden_path_llm_2024} provide direct evidence that LLMs exhibit analogous garden-path effects. At the pragmatic level, \\citet{hu_pragmatic_2023} and \\citet{ruis_goldilocks_2023} show that LLMs struggle with implicature resolution, defaulting to literal interpretations even when pragmatic inference is required. \\citet{liu_ambiguity_2023} demonstrate that language models fail to represent ambiguity, collapsing multiple interpretations into a single output; \\citet{gur_arieh_ambiguity_collapse_2026} taxonomize the epistemic risks of this ``ambiguity collapse''; \\citet{sedova_to_know_2024} show analogous self-inconsistencies under entity-type ambiguity. These findings ground our \\emph{Disambiguation Neglect} result: agents process ambiguous input without meta-cognitive awareness but, unlike humans, lack social channels to recover. \\citet{disambiguation_cqa_survey_2025} survey disambiguation strategies in conversational QA; \\citet{suri_structured_2025} show that structured uncertainty estimation improves coverage by 7--39\\%, while \\citet{sehwag_aspi_2026} demonstrate that clarification-seeking introduces prompt injection vulnerabilities---a tension for disambiguation channel design.\n\n\\paragraph{Agent safety evaluation.}\nThe agent safety evaluation landscape includes benchmarks for risk awareness \\citep{rjudge_2024}, tool-use safety \\citep{toolemu_2024}, indirect prompt injection \\citep{injecagent_2024}, safe task planning \\citep{yin_safeagentbench_2025}, policy-invisible violations \\citep{phantompolicy_2026}, comprehensive real-world agent safety \\citep{vijayvargiya_openagentsafety_2026}, trajectory-level diagnosis \\citep{atbench_2026}, and deployment-condition probing \\citep{riskybench_2026}. \\citet{tamkin_evaluating_2023} evaluate how language models navigate policy-governed decisions, \\citet{shieldagent_2025} extract formal safety rules from policy documents, and AgentAuditor \\citep{agentauditor_2025} achieves human-level safety evaluation. Our work differs by focusing on the \\emph{source specification} rather than the agent's execution: we measure how ambiguity in the policy itself causes safety violations, complementing work that assumes clean policies.\n\n\\paragraph{Policy specification for agents.}\nDRIP-R \\citep{drip_r_2026} is the closest concurrent work: it benchmarks 400 policy-ambiguous retail scenarios across five RE ambiguity types, measuring decision quality rather than safety violations. Our work differs in three respects: (1) our matched-pair design establishes causality rather than correlating ambiguity presence with outcomes; (2) we measure safety violations rather than decision quality, enabling failure mechanism analysis; (3) our two-layer taxonomy adds linguistic-layer types, and we quantify the model$\\times$type interaction. We map DRIP-R's five RE ambiguity categories to our six-type taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:drip_mapping}. PolicyBank \\citep{policybank_2026} studies iterative policy refinement, closing 82\\% of the gap to a human oracle. AgentPex \\citep{agentpex_2026} detects procedural violations in agentic traces, finding that 83\\% of perfect-outcome traces contain violations. MANTRA \\citep{mantra_2026} synthesizes SMT-validated compliance benchmarks from procedural manuals. $\\tau$-bench \\citep{tau_bench_2024} and its successor $\\tau^2$-bench \\citep{tau2_bench_2025} provide the conversational agent evaluation platforms. ",
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Ambiguity in NLP and requirements engineering.}\nAmbiguity has been studied extensively in both computational linguistics and software engineering. Word sense disambiguation \\citep{navigli_wsd_2009} addresses lexical ambiguity at the token level; our lexical type extends this to policy-specific polysemy. \\citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} propose an 11-type NLP taxonomy covering syntactic, semantic, and pragmatic ambiguity, from which we draw our linguistic-layer types. \\citet{kamath_scope_2024} investigate scopal ambiguity in LLMs, finding systematic preferences for surface scope. In requirements engineering (RE), \\citet{massey_ambiguity_re_2014} classify six ambiguity types in regulatory texts, and \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004} introduce the distinction between nocuous ambiguity (where stakeholders diverge on interpretation) and innocuous ambiguity (where they converge). Computational detection of nocuous ambiguity has progressed from heuristic methods \\citep{chantree_2006,willis_nocuous_2008} through NLP-based cross-domain detectors \\citep{ferrari_ambiguity_2019,ferrari_defects_2018} to transformer-based approaches for anaphoric ambiguity \\citep{ezzini_taphsir_2022,ezzini_anaphoric_2022}. \\citet{zhao_nlp4re_2021} survey the broader NLP4RE landscape. Our work extends Berry and Kamsties' framework to LLM agents, where the absence of shared organizational context makes previously innocuous ambiguity nocuous. Our taxonomy serves as an experimental instrument for controlled causal analysis rather than a proposed classification standard.\n\n\\paragraph{Psycholinguistics of ambiguity processing.}\n\\citet{ferreira_good_enough_2002} show that human comprehenders build shallow, ``good-enough'' representations rather than full parses, paralleling our observation that LLM agents adopt surface interpretations. \\citet{frazier_rayner_1982} demonstrate early commitment to a single parse; \\citet{li_garden_path_llm_2024} provide direct evidence that LLMs exhibit analogous garden-path effects. At the pragmatic level, \\citet{hu_pragmatic_2023} and \\citet{ruis_goldilocks_2023} show that LLMs struggle with implicature resolution, defaulting to literal interpretations even when pragmatic inference is required. \\citet{liu_ambiguity_2023} demonstrate that language models fail to represent ambiguity, collapsing multiple interpretations into a single output; \\citet{gur_arieh_ambiguity_collapse_2026} taxonomize the epistemic risks of this ``ambiguity collapse''; \\citet{sedova_to_know_2024} show analogous self-inconsistencies under entity-type ambiguity. These findings ground our \\emph{disambiguation neglect} result: agents process ambiguous input without meta-cognitive awareness but, unlike humans, lack social channels to recover. \\citet{disambiguation_cqa_survey_2025} survey disambiguation strategies in conversational QA; \\citet{suri_structured_2025} show that structured uncertainty estimation improves coverage by 7--39\\%, while \\citet{sehwag_aspi_2026} demonstrate that clarification-seeking introduces prompt injection vulnerabilities---a tension for disambiguation channel design.\n\n\\paragraph{Agent safety evaluation.}\nThe agent safety evaluation landscape includes benchmarks for risk awareness \\citep{rjudge_2024}, tool-use safety \\citep{toolemu_2024}, indirect prompt injection \\citep{injecagent_2024}, safe task planning \\citep{yin_safeagentbench_2025}, policy-invisible violations \\citep{phantompolicy_2026}, comprehensive real-world agent safety \\citep{vijayvargiya_openagentsafety_2026}, trajectory-level diagnosis \\citep{atbench_2026}, and deployment-condition probing \\citep{riskybench_2026}. \\citet{tamkin_evaluating_2023} evaluate how language models navigate policy-governed decisions, \\citet{shieldagent_2025} extract formal safety rules from policy documents, and AgentAuditor \\citep{agentauditor_2025} achieves human-level safety evaluation. Our work differs by focusing on the \\emph{source specification} rather than the agent's execution: we measure how ambiguity in the policy itself causes safety violations, complementing work that assumes clean policies.\n\n\\paragraph{Policy specification for agents.}\nDRIP-R \\citep{drip_r_2026} is the closest concurrent work: it benchmarks 400 policy-ambiguous retail scenarios across five RE ambiguity types, measuring decision quality rather than safety violations. Our work differs in three respects: (1) our matched-pair design establishes causality rather than correlating ambiguity presence with outcomes; (2) we measure safety violations rather than decision quality, enabling failure mechanism analysis; (3) our two-layer taxonomy adds linguistic-layer types, and we quantify the model$\\times$type interaction. We map DRIP-R's five RE ambiguity categories to our six-type taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:drip_mapping}. PolicyBank \\citep{policybank_2026} studies iterative policy refinement, closing 82\\% of the gap to a human oracle. AgentPex \\citep{agentpex_2026} detects procedural violations in agentic traces, finding that 83\\% of perfect-outcome traces contain violations. MANTRA \\citep{mantra_2026} synthesizes SMT-validated compliance benchmarks from procedural manuals. $\\tau$-bench \\citep{tau_bench_2024} and its successor $\\tau^2$-bench \\citep{tau2_bench_2025} provide the conversational agent evaluation platforms. ",

        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " \\citet{ferreira_good_enough_2002} show that human comprehenders build shallow, ``good-enough'' representations rather than full parses, paralleling our observation that LLM agents adopt surface interpretations. \\citet{frazier_rayner_1982} demonstrate early commitment to a single parse; \\citet{li_garden_path_llm_2024} provide direct evidence that LLMs exhibit analogous garden-path effects. At the pragmatic level, \\citet{hu_pragmatic_2023} and \\citet{ruis_goldilocks_2023} show that LLMs struggle with implicature resolution, defaulting to literal interpretations even when pragmatic inference is required. \\citet{liu_ambiguity_2023} demonstrate that language models fail to represent ambiguity, collapsing multiple interpretations into a single output; \\citet{gur_arieh_ambiguity_collapse_2026} taxonomize the epistemic risks of this ``ambiguity collapse''; \\citet{sedova_to_know_2024} show analogous self-inconsistencies under entity-type ambiguity. These findings ground our \\emph{disambiguation neglect} result: agents process ambiguous input without meta-cognitive awareness but, unlike humans, lack social channels to recover. \\citet{disambiguation_cqa_survey_2025} survey disambiguation strategies in conversational QA; \\citet{suri_structured_2025} show that structured uncertainty estimation improves coverage by 7--39\\%, while \\citet{sehwag_aspi_2026} demonstrate that clarification-seeking introduces prompt injection vulnerabilities---a tension for disambiguation channel design.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Agent safety evaluation.}",
          "-The agent safety evaluation landscape includes benchmarks for risk awareness \\citep{rjudge_2024}, tool-use safety \\citep{toolemu_2024}, indirect prompt injection \\citep{injecagent_2024}, safe task planning \\citep{yin_safeagentbench_2025}, policy-invisible violations \\citep{phantompolicy_2026}, comprehensive real-world agent safety \\citep{vijayvargiya_openagentsafety_2026}, trajectory-level diagnosis \\citep{atbench_2026}, and deployment-condition probing \\citep{riskybench_2026}. \\citet{tamkin_evaluating_2023} evaluate how language models navigate policy-governed decisions, \\citet{shieldagent_2025} extract formal safety rules from policy documents, and AgentAuditor \\citep{agentauditor_2025} achieves human-level safety evaluation. Our work differs by focusing on the \\emph{source specification} rather than the agent's execution: we measure how ambiguity in the policy itself causes safety violations, complementing work that assumes clean policies.",
          "+The agent safety evaluation landscape includes benchmarks for risk awareness \\citep{rjudge_2024}, tool-use safety \\citep{toolemu_2024}, indirect prompt injection \\citep{injecagent_2024}, safe task planning \\citep{yin_safeagentbench_2025}, policy-invisible violations \\citep{phantompolicy_2026}, comprehensive real-world agent safety \\citep{vijayvargiya_openagentsafety_2026}, trajectory-level diagnosis \\citep{atbench_2026}, and deployment-condition probing \\citep{riskybench_2026}. \\citet{mu_can_llms_2023} evaluate rule-following under policy constraints, \\citet{tamkin_evaluating_2023} examine how language models navigate policy-governed decisions, \\citet{shieldagent_2025} extract formal safety rules from policy documents, and AgentAuditor \\citep{agentauditor_2025} achieves human-level safety evaluation. Our work differs by focusing on the \\emph{source specification} rather than the agent's execution: we measure how ambiguity in the policy itself causes safety violations, complementing work that assumes clean policies.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Policy specification for agents.}",
          " DRIP-R \\citep{drip_r_2026} is the closest concurrent work: it benchmarks 400 policy-ambiguous retail scenarios across five RE ambiguity types, measuring decision quality rather than safety violations. Our work differs in three respects: (1) our matched-pair design establishes causality rather than correlating ambiguity presence with outcomes; (2) we measure safety violations rather than decision quality, enabling failure mechanism analysis; (3) our two-layer taxonomy adds linguistic-layer types, and we quantify the model$\\times$type interaction. We map DRIP-R's five RE ambiguity categories to our six-type taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:drip_mapping}. PolicyBank \\citep{policybank_2026} studies iterative policy refinement, closing 82\\% of the gap to a human oracle. AgentPex \\citep{agentpex_2026} detects procedural violations in agentic traces, finding that 83\\% of perfect-outcome traces contain violations. MANTRA \\citep{mantra_2026} synthesizes SMT-validated compliance benchmarks from procedural manuals. $\\tau$-bench \\citep{tau_bench_2024} and its successor $\\tau^2$-bench \\citep{tau2_bench_2025} provide the conversational agent evaluation platforms. ",
          "\\ No newline at end of file"
        ]
      }
    ],
    "userModified": false,
    "replaceAll": false
  },
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Differential nocuity.}\nBuilding on \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}'s nocuous/innocuous distinction, our results support a \\emph{differential nocuity} hypothesis \\citep{willis_nocuous_2008}: risk depends on type, safety training, and disambiguation channel availability. The binary effect is domain-robust (interaction $p = 0.187$) but type rankings are not ($\\rho = 0.26$), suggesting nocuity is universal while its type-specific profile requires per-domain calibration. Nevertheless, 60.8\\% of violation variance remains unexplained, reflecting conversation trajectory and user-simulator stochasticity; clause-level auditing addresses the largest controllable factor (22.4\\%) but does not eliminate deployment-time randomness.\n\n\\paragraph{Taxonomy as diagnostic tool.}\nType membership explains only 0.4\\% of violation variance versus 22.4\\% for clause wording; however, it predicts failure \\emph{mechanism} 17$\\times$ more strongly than violation rate (NMI ratio $\\approx 17$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), validating the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-based mitigation.}\nA type-specific mitigation study (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) finds prompting effective for authorization scope ($-18.0$\\,pp) and lexical ($-8.9$\\,pp) but not incompleteness ($-3.3$\\,pp, reversing across formulations), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes \\citep{chantree_2006,ferrari_ambiguity_2019}.\n",

          "+Building on \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}'s nocuous/innocuous distinction, our results support a \\emph{differential nocuity} hypothesis \\citep{willis_nocuous_2008}: risk depends on type, safety training, and disambiguation channel availability. The binary effect is domain-robust (interaction $p = 0.187$) but type rankings are not ($\\rho = 0.26$), suggesting nocuity is universal while its type-specific profile requires per-domain calibration. Nevertheless, 60.8\\% of variance remains unexplained (conversation trajectory, user-simulator stochasticity); clause-level auditing targets the largest controllable factor (22.4\\%).",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Taxonomy as diagnostic tool.}",
          " Type membership explains only 0.4\\% of violation variance versus 22.4\\% for clause wording; however, it predicts failure \\emph{mechanism} 17$\\times$ more strongly than violation rate (NMI ratio $\\approx 17$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), validating the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis})."
        ]
      }
```

```json
    "newString": "Replication on two additional model families confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); a cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency ($+13.8$\\,pp pooled, 3/5 significant; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around four analyses of increasing specificity: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2), the aggregate layer effect (C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 constitute our primary claims; C3 is inconclusive; C4 is exploratory but provides the model$\\times$type interaction analysis.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity ($n = 300$, $\\Delta_{\\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\\,pp, CI containing zero). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\\footnote{Type III SS on binary outcomes approximates a linear probability model; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant factor ordering (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}), though magnitudes differ between linear and logistic specifications.} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), model 1.0\\%, condition 15.8\\%, residual 60.8\\% (conversation trajectory, user-simulator stochasticity). A permutation test ($n = 1{,}000$, shuffling within type; Appendix~\\ref{app:clause_variance}) confirms the clause effect exceeds chance (null mean $= 8.3\\%$, $p < 0.001$); clause-level ICC $= 0.386$ under ambiguous conditions (full-data ICC $= 0.189$). Individual clause wording---not type membership---drives clause-level variance; the omnibus test on ambiguous episodes nonetheless confirms significant type heterogeneity ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $V = 0.137$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\\,pp) is an upper bound that includes information addition; the confound-controlled range is $+$18.7\\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\\,pp (authorization scope; Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}).\n\nReplication on two additional model families confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp, both $p < 10^{-6}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); a cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency across all five models ($+13.8$\\,pp pooled; three individually significant at $p < 0.05$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy's primary value lies not in predicting clause risk but in predicting failure mechanisms: type predicts mechanism 17$\\times$ more strongly than violation rate (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Tier: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Tier & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). TOST equivalence testing at a $\\pm 15$ pp margin reveals a graded equivalence structure among five types---authorization scope (41.6\\%), lexical (42.8\\%), scopal (39.4\\%), and conditional precedence (36.8\\%) form a mid-tier cluster, with coreferential (31.6\\%) below---while incompleteness (53.6\\%) stands apart as an upper bound---conditional on the confound controls in Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}---whose ranking partly reflects an information-addition confound. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nSensitivity analysis across seven margins ($\\pm 5$--$20$ pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}) confirms this structure is margin-dependent: mid-tier cohesion emerges at $\\pm 12$ pp and stabilizes at $\\pm 15$ pp, while the incompleteness upper-bound is robust across all margins. A bootstrap cluster analysis ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) corroborates: incompleteness forms a singleton at $k = 2$, coreferential additionally separates at $k = 3$. Cross-model type ranking concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}), driven by incompleteness ranking highest in four of five models.\n\nAn important qualification: cross-judge-group type rankings are discordant ($\\rho = 0.09$, $p = 0.87$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), driven by authorization scope ranking first in one judge group but last in the other. Excluding it, the remaining five types show strong concordance ($\\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$). C2 should be interpreted as establishing robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound---rather than a complete linear ordering.\n\nIncompleteness is highest in four of five models (rates $\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}). Its unambiguous variant adds policy content, so the 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound; a confound analysis confirms significance after controlling for information delta (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). Coreferential, whose variants are lexically identical, provides a confound-free lower bound at 31.6\\%.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Layer Effect (C3)}\n\nSpecification-layer types trend higher than linguistic-layer types (44.0\\% vs.\\ 37.9\\%, $p = 0.017$), but C3 is inconclusive: the effect does not survive Holm--Bonferroni correction and controlling for token delta reverses the direction. We treat the two-layer structure as organizational convenience rather than a theoretical claim.\n\n\\subsection{Model $\\times$ Type Interaction (C4) \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:c4}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) by model and type under ambiguous conditions ($n = 2{,}997$). Authorization scope varies 52\\,pp across models ($\\kappa = 0.385$); incompleteness remains uniformly high (46--62\\%). C4 interaction is authorization-scope-specific (excluding it: $p = 0.97$). Each model was evaluated by a single judge; C4 patterns should be interpreted cautiously.}\n\\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nA model main effect is significant ($\\chi^2 = 31.02$, $p = 3.0 \\times 10^{-6}$, $V = 0.102$), with Claude (30.3\\%) substantially less vulnerable than DeepSeek (49.7\\%). Model identity explains only 1.0\\% of total violation variance; the significant interaction ($F(20, 2976) = 1.97$, $p = 0.006$; $\\mathrm{df} = (5{-}1) \\times (6{-}1) = 20$; 2.9\\% of variance; confirmed by logistic regression likelihood ratio test: $\\chi^2(20) = 47.20$, $p = 5.5 \\times 10^{-4}$) concentrates in specific type--model combinations.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:heatmap} highlights three patterns: (1) authorization scope is the \\emph{most model-differentiating type} (52\\,pp range; Claude 12\\% to GPT-5.4 64\\%), though its low judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$) adds uncertainty; (2) incompleteness is a \\emph{consistent vulnerability} (46--62\\%); (3) conditional precedence is the most \\emph{model-invariant} (34--40\\%). The authorization scope ranking is stable within the GPT-5.4 judge subset (bootstrap preservation: 99.5\\%), and the 34\\,pp within-judge range far exceeds measurement noise; given the low cross-judge $\\kappa$, we characterize this as suggestive given the single-judge-per-model design (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}). Excluding authorization scope, the residual interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}).\n\n",
```

```json
    "newString": "A mitigation study (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) finds prompting effective for authorization scope ($-18.0$\\,pp) and lexical ($-8.9$\\,pp) but not incompleteness ($-3.3$\\,pp), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes \\citep{chantree_2006,ferrari_ambiguity_2019}.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Differential nocuity.}\nBuilding on \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}'s nocuous/innocuous distinction, our results support a \\emph{differential nocuity} hypothesis \\citep{willis_nocuous_2008}: risk depends on type, safety training, and disambiguation channel availability. The binary effect is domain-robust (interaction $p = 0.187$) but type rankings are not ($\\rho = 0.26$), suggesting nocuity is universal while its type-specific profile requires per-domain calibration. Nevertheless, 60.8\\% of variance remains unexplained (conversation trajectory, user-simulator stochasticity); clause-level auditing targets the largest controllable factor (22.4\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Taxonomy as diagnostic tool.}\nType membership explains only 0.4\\% of violation variance versus 22.4\\% for clause wording; however, it predicts failure \\emph{mechanism} 17$\\times$ more strongly than violation rate (NMI ratio $\\approx 17$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), validating the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-based mitigation.}\nA type-specific mitigation study (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) finds prompting effective for authorization scope ($-18.0$\\,pp) and lexical ($-8.9$\\,pp) but not incompleteness ($-3.3$\\,pp, reversing across formulations), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes \\citep{chantree_2006,ferrari_ambiguity_2019}.\n",
```

### User

_source line 4253 · 2026-05-22T21:19:49.790Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The user is the AI execution layer (Main Agent) for the `policy_ambiguity_safety` EMNLP 2026 paper project. The Director issues instructions; the Agent coordinates Workers and reports back. This session covered:
   - **Permutation test**: Fix a buggy script, run it, integrate results into the paper
   - **Batch B text edits (R17)**: 30×→17× NMI replacement, ICC/permutation text, threshold sensitivity, various limitations additions, Tjur citation
   - **SOPBench Phase 2C**: Monitor 5-model experiment completion, analyze results, write appendix section
   - **R18 major framing adjustments**: 4 MF items (C2 demotion, judge-model confound, taxonomy demotion, DN demotion), multiple should-fix items, page compression
   - **Page budget management**: Keep main text ≤ 8 pages throughout all edits (EMNLP 2026 hard constraint)

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - EMNLP 2026 ARR submission: strict 8-page main text limit (Limitations/references unlimited)
   - τ²-bench agent safety benchmark (retail + airline customer service)
   - Matched-pair causal design: 300 clause pairs × 2 conditions × 5 models = 2,997 episodes
   - Variance decomposition: clause 22.4%, type 0.4%, model 1.0%, condition 15.8%, residual 60.8%
   - NMI ratio: 0.2316/0.0138 = 16.78 ≈ 17× (replaced raw MI ratio of 30.2×)
   - Permutation test: shuffle violation_rate within (type, condition, model) strata, 1000 iterations
   - Permutation results: observed R²=22.4%, null mean=8.3%, SD=0.6%, 95th=9.4%, p<0.001
   - ICC: 0.386 (ambiguous condition), 0.189 (mixed-model/full-data)
   - Systematic nocuity thresholds: ≥2/5: 54.7%, ≥3/5: 41.3%, ≥4/5: 23.7%, 5/5: 13.0%
   - SOPBench cross-benchmark replication: 47 clause pairs × 4 domains × 5 models
   - SOPBench pooled: Δ=+13.8pp, OR=1.95, p<10⁻⁶ (all 5 models directionally consistent)
   - McNemar conditional OR = 15.1, n_disc = 580 of 1,497 pairs

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`docs/paper/sections/abstract.tex`**
     - Central to N1 (C2 demotion), W7 (number pruning), M6 (nocuous definition), N4 (DN lowercase)
     - Current state after R18: removed "2,997 episodes", "1.0% for model identity", compressed C2 to "Per-type effects range from +18.7 to +49.2pp, with incompleteness as the consistent upper bound", "disambiguation neglect" lowercase, added "consistently nocuous (i.e., harmful)"

   - **`docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex`**
     - N1: Contribution (2) rewritten to: "(2) a variance decomposition revealing that clause wording explains 22.4% of violation variance versus 0.4% for type, combined with a mutual information analysis showing that type predicts failure mechanism 17× better than violation rate, motivating type-level analysis as a diagnostic rather than predictive tool"
     - N3: Contribution (3) compressed to: "(3) an error analysis revealing type-specific failure mechanisms and convergent violation patterns across models"

4. Errors and Fixes:
   - **Permutation test bug (critical)**: Original script used bijective label permutation (clause_map = dict(zip(clauses, shuffled_clauses))), which just renamed groups without changing group membership. R² is invariant to label permutation, so null R² = observed R². Fixed by shuffling violation_rate values within each (ambiguity_type, condition, model) stratum, correctly breaking clause-to-outcome correlation. After fix: null R² ≈ 8.3% vs observed 22.4%.
   - **Page overflow (recurring)**: Every round of text additions pushed Conclusion past page 8. Fixed through iterative compress-compile-verify cycles: compressed §6.3, §7, §5.1 permutation text, §6.2 DN, conclusion, mitigation paragraph, replication paragraph, W2 residual sentence. Removed "and model (1.0%)" from conclusion.

   - **SOPBench result field**: Initially used wrong field (cnv/success). Correct field is `evaluation.constraint_not_violated` (False = violation).

```json
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Differential nocuity.}\nBuilding on \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}'s nocuous/innocuous distinction, our results support a \\emph{differential nocuity} hypothesis \\citep{willis_nocuous_2008}: risk depends on type, safety training, and disambiguation channel availability. The binary effect is domain-robust (interaction $p = 0.187$) but type rankings are not ($\\rho = 0.26$), suggesting nocuity is universal while its type-specific profile requires per-domain calibration. Nevertheless, 60.8\\% of variance remains unexplained (conversation trajectory, user-simulator stochasticity); clause-level auditing targets the largest controllable factor (22.4\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{Taxonomy as diagnostic tool.}\nType membership explains only 0.4\\% of violation variance versus 22.4\\% for clause wording; however, it predicts failure \\emph{mechanism} 17$\\times$ more strongly than violation rate (NMI ratio $\\approx 17$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), validating the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-based mitigation.}\nA mitigation study (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) finds prompting effective for authorization scope ($-18.0$\\,pp) and lexical ($-8.9$\\,pp) but not incompleteness ($-3.3$\\,pp), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes \\citep{chantree_2006,ferrari_ambiguity_2019}.\n",
        "numLines": 12,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 12
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex"
```

```json
        "content": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around four analyses of increasing specificity: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2), the aggregate layer effect (C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 constitute our primary claims; C3 is inconclusive; C4 is exploratory but provides the model$\\times$type interaction analysis.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity ($n = 300$, $\\Delta_{\\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\\,pp, CI containing zero). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\\footnote{Type III SS on binary outcomes approximates a linear probability model; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant factor ordering (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}), though magnitudes differ between linear and logistic specifications.} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), model 1.0\\%, condition 15.8\\%, residual 60.8\\% (conversation trajectory, user-simulator stochasticity). A permutation test ($n = 1{,}000$, shuffling within type; Appendix~\\ref{app:clause_variance}) confirms the clause effect exceeds chance (null mean $= 8.3\\%$, $p < 0.001$); clause-level ICC $= 0.386$ under ambiguous conditions (full-data ICC $= 0.189$). Individual clause wording---not type membership---drives clause-level variance; the omnibus test on ambiguous episodes nonetheless confirms significant type heterogeneity ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $V = 0.137$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\\,pp) is an upper bound that includes information addition; the confound-controlled range is $+$18.7\\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\\,pp (authorization scope; Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}).\n\nReplication on two additional model families confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); a cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency ($+13.8$\\,pp pooled, 3/5 significant; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy's primary value lies not in predicting clause risk but in predicting failure mechanisms: type predicts mechanism 17$\\times$ more strongly than violation rate (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Tier: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Tier & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). TOST equivalence testing at a $\\pm 15$ pp margin reveals a graded equivalence structure among five types---authorization scope (41.6\\%), lexical (42.8\\%), scopal (39.4\\%), and conditional precedence (36.8\\%) form a mid-tier cluster, with coreferential (31.6\\%) below---while incompleteness (53.6\\%) stands apart as an upper bound---conditional on the confound controls in Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}---whose ranking partly reflects an information-addition confound. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nSensitivity analysis across seven margins ($\\pm 5$--$20$ pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}) confirms this structure is margin-dependent: mid-tier cohesion emerges at $\\pm 12$ pp and stabilizes at $\\pm 15$ pp, while the incompleteness upper-bound is robust across all margins. A bootstrap cluster analysis ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) corroborates: incompleteness forms a singleton at $k = 2$, coreferential additionally separates at $k = 3$. Cross-model type ranking concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}), driven by incompleteness ranking highest in four of five models.\n\nAn important qualification: cross-judge-group type rankings are discordant ($\\rho = 0.09$, $p = 0.87$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), driven by authorization scope ranking first in one judge group but last in the other. Excluding it, the remaining five types show strong concordance ($\\rho = 0.90$, $p = 0.037$). C2 should be interpreted as establishing robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound---rather than a complete linear ordering.\n\nIncompleteness is highest in four of five models (rates $\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}). Its unambiguous variant adds policy content, so the 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound; a confound analysis confirms significance after controlling for information delta (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). Coreferential, whose variants are lexically identical, provides a confound-free lower bound at 31.6\\%.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Layer Effect (C3)}\n\nSpecification-layer types trend higher than linguistic-layer types (44.0\\% vs.\\ 37.9\\%, $p = 0.017$), but C3 is inconclusive: the effect does not survive Holm--Bonferroni correction and controlling for token delta reverses the direction. We treat the two-layer structure as organizational convenience rather than a theoretical claim.\n\n\\subsection{Model $\\times$ Type Interaction (C4) \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:c4}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) by model and type under ambiguous conditions ($n = 2{,}997$). Authorization scope varies 52\\,pp across models ($\\kappa = 0.385$); incompleteness remains uniformly high (46--62\\%). C4 interaction is authorization-scope-specific (excluding it: $p = 0.97$). Each model was evaluated by a single judge; C4 patterns should be interpreted cautiously.}\n\\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nA model main effect is significant ($\\chi^2 = 31.02$, $p = 3.0 \\times 10^{-6}$, $V = 0.102$), with Claude (30.3\\%) substantially less vulnerable than DeepSeek (49.7\\%). Model identity explains only 1.0\\% of total violation variance; the significant interaction ($F(20, 2976) = 1.97$, $p = 0.006$; $\\mathrm{df} = (5{-}1) \\times (6{-}1) = 20$; 2.9\\% of variance; confirmed by logistic regression likelihood ratio test: $\\chi^2(20) = 47.20$, $p = 5.5 \\times 10^{-4}$) concentrates in specific type--model combinations.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:heatmap} highlights three patterns: (1) authorization scope is the \\emph{most model-differentiating type} (52\\,pp range; Claude 12\\% to GPT-5.4 64\\%), though its low judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$) adds uncertainty; (2) incompleteness is a \\emph{consistent vulnerability} (46--62\\%); (3) conditional precedence is the most \\emph{model-invariant} (34--40\\%). The authorization scope ranking is stable within the GPT-5.4 judge subset (bootstrap preservation: 99.5\\%), and the 34\\,pp within-judge range far exceeds measurement noise; given the low cross-judge $\\kappa$, we characterize this as suggestive given the single-judge-per-model design (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}). Excluding authorization scope, the residual interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}).\n\n",
        "numLines": 70,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 70
      }
    },
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "5d1eeb5f-6507-4d0f-badb-1d63b7dede9d",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Ambiguity in NLP and requirements engineering.}\nAmbiguity has been studied extensively in both computational linguistics and software engineering. Word sense disambiguation \\citep{navigli_wsd_2009} addresses lexical ambiguity at the token level; our lexical type extends this to policy-specific polysemy. \\citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} propose an 11-type NLP taxonomy covering syntactic, semantic, and pragmatic ambiguity, from which we draw our linguistic-layer types. \\citet{kamath_scope_2024} investigate scopal ambiguity in LLMs, finding systematic preferences for surface scope. In requirements engineering (RE), \\citet{massey_ambiguity_re_2014} classify six ambiguity types in regulatory texts, and \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004} introduce the distinction between nocuous ambiguity (where stakeholders diverge on interpretation) and innocuous ambiguity (where they converge). Computational detection of nocuous ambiguity has progressed from heuristic methods \\citep{chantree_2006,willis_nocuous_2008} through NLP-based cross-domain detectors \\citep{ferrari_ambiguity_2019,ferrari_defects_2018} to transformer-based approaches for anaphoric ambiguity \\citep{ezzini_taphsir_2022,ezzini_anaphoric_2022}. \\citet{zhao_nlp4re_2021} survey the broader NLP4RE landscape. Our work extends Berry and Kamsties' framework to LLM agents, where the absence of shared organizational context makes previously innocuous ambiguity nocuous. Our taxonomy serves as an experimental instrument for controlled causal analysis rather than a proposed classification standard.\n\n\\paragraph{Psycholinguistics of ambiguity processing.}\n\\citet{ferreira_good_enough_2002} show that human comprehenders build shallow, ``good-enough'' representations rather than full parses, paralleling our observation that LLM agents adopt surface interpretations. \\citet{frazier_rayner_1982} demonstrate early commitment to a single parse; \\citet{li_garden_path_llm_2024} provide direct evidence that LLMs exhibit analogous garden-path effects. At the pragmatic level, \\citet{hu_pragmatic_2023} and \\citet{ruis_goldilocks_2023} show that LLMs struggle with implicature resolution, defaulting to literal interpretations even when pragmatic inference is required. \\citet{liu_ambiguity_2023} demonstrate that language models fail to represent ambiguity, collapsing multiple interpretations into a single output; \\citet{gur_arieh_ambiguity_collapse_2026} taxonomize the epistemic risks of this ``ambiguity collapse''; \\citet{sedova_to_know_2024} show analogous self-inconsistencies under entity-type ambiguity. These findings ground our \\emph{disambiguation neglect} result: agents process ambiguous input without meta-cognitive awareness but, unlike humans, lack social channels to recover. \\citet{disambiguation_cqa_survey_2025} survey disambiguation strategies in conversational QA; \\citet{suri_structured_2025} show that structured uncertainty estimation improves coverage by 7--39\\%, while \\citet{sehwag_aspi_2026} demonstrate that clarification-seeking introduces prompt injection vulnerabilities---a tension for disambiguation channel design.\n\n\\paragraph{Agent safety evaluation.}\nThe agent safety evaluation landscape includes benchmarks for risk awareness \\citep{rjudge_2024}, tool-use safety \\citep{toolemu_2024}, indirect prompt injection \\citep{injecagent_2024}, safe task planning \\citep{yin_safeagentbench_2025}, policy-invisible violations \\citep{phantompolicy_2026}, comprehensive real-world agent safety \\citep{vijayvargiya_openagentsafety_2026}, trajectory-level diagnosis \\citep{atbench_2026}, and deployment-condition probing \\citep{riskybench_2026}. \\citet{mu_can_llms_2023} evaluate rule-following under policy constraints, \\citet{tamkin_evaluating_2023} examine how language models navigate policy-governed decisions, \\citet{shieldagent_2025} extract formal safety rules from policy documents, and AgentAuditor \\citep{agentauditor_2025} achieves human-level safety evaluation. Our work differs by focusing on the \\emph{source specification} rather than the agent's execution: we measure how ambiguity in the policy itself causes safety violations, complementing work that assumes clean policies.\n\n\\paragraph{Policy specification for agents.}\nDRIP-R \\citep{drip_r_2026} is the closest concurrent work: it benchmarks 400 policy-ambiguous retail scenarios across five RE ambiguity types, measuring decision quality rather than safety violations. Our work differs in three respects: (1) our matched-pair design establishes causality rather than correlating ambiguity presence with outcomes; (2) we measure safety violations rather than decision quality, enabling failure mechanism analysis; (3) our two-layer taxonomy adds linguistic-layer types, and we quantify the model$\\times$type interaction. We map DRIP-R's five RE ambiguity categories to our six-type taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:drip_mapping}. PolicyBank \\citep{policybank_2026} studies iterative policy refinement, closing 82\\% of the gap to a human oracle. AgentPex \\citep{agentpex_2026} detects procedural violations in agentic traces, finding that 83\\% of perfect-outcome traces contain violations. MANTRA \\citep{mantra_2026} synthesizes SMT-validated compliance benchmarks from procedural manuals. $\\tau$-bench \\citep{tau_bench_2024} and its successor $\\tau^2$-bench \\citep{tau2_bench_2025} provide the conversational agent evaluation platforms. ",
        "numLines": 14,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 14
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex"
  },
```
